\chapteropen[\href{https://www.hodinkee.com/articles/patek-philippe-caliber-89-easter-problem}{The astronomical clock in Notre-Dame-de-Strasbourg Cathedral, for computing the date of Easter.  Easter falls on the first Sunday after the full moon on or after after the spring equinox. Calculation of this date was a great challenge for mechanisms of that time, 1843.}]{automata/pix/easterclock.jpg}{Automata} %https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:CIMA_mg_8332.jpg
% Use as much of http://cstheory.stackexchange.com/a/14818/4731 as possible

Our touchstone is the Turing machine.
To better understand it we can dissamble it.
A Turing machines has only two components, a CPU and a tape.
Consequently, in this section we take the tape away and
study the CPU alone.

Alternatively stated,
while a Turing Machine has unbounded memory, 
the devices that we use every day do not.
We can ask \citetext{what jobs can be done by a machine with bounded memory}{%
  From Rabin, Scott, Finite Automata and Their Decision Problems, 1959:
  \textit{Turing machines are widely considered to be the abstract prototype
  of digital computers; workers in the field, however, have felt more and more
  that the notion of a Turing machine is too general to serve as an accurate
  model of actual computers. It is well known that even for simple
  calculations it is impossible to give an a priori upper bound on the amount
  of tape a Turing machine will need for any given computation.
  It is precisely this feature that renders Turing's concept unrealistic.
  % 
  In the last few years the idea of a finite automaton has appeared in the
  literature. These are machines having only a finite number of internal
  states that can be used for memory and computation. The restriction on
  finiteness appears to give a better approximation to the idea of a physical
  machine. Of course, such machines cannot do as much as Turing machines,
  but the advantage of being able to compute an arbitrary general recursive
  function is questionable, since very few of these functions come up in
  practical applications.} % http://cstheory.stackexchange.com/a/14816
  }.



\section{Finite State Machines}
We produce a new model of computation by modifying
the definition of Turing Machine.
We will strip out the capability to write, changing the
tape head from read/write to read-only.
This gives us insight into what can be done with states alone.
It will turn out that this type of machine can do many things, but
not as many as a Turing machine.

\subsection{Definition}  
We will use the same type of transition tables and transition graphs
as with Turing machines.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:PowerSwitch}
A power switch has two states, $q_{\text{off}}$ and $q_{\text{on}}$ and
its input alphabet has one symbol, \str{toggle}.

\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:Turnstile}
Operate this turnstile by puting in two tokens and then pushing through.
It has three states and
its input alphabet is $\Sigma=\set{\str{token},\str{push}}$.
\begin{center}
  % \newlength{\scratchheight}
  % \newsavebox{\scratchbox}
  \savebox{\scratchbox}{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa01.pdf}}
  \settoheight{\jhscratchheight}{\usebox{\scratchbox}}

  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=0.925\jhscratchheight]{automata/pix/turnstile2.jpg}} % http://turnstiles.us/hwd/catrax_plus_foto3.jpg % http://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/2c/Turnstile.alewife.agr.jpg
\end{center}
\end{example}

As we saw with Turing machines, the states are a limited form of memory.
For instance, $q_{\text{one}}$ is how the turnstile ``remembers'' that it
has so far received one token.

\begin{example} \label{ex:VendingMachine}
This vending machine dispenses items that cost $30$~cents.\footnotemark[1]
The picture is complex so we will show it in three layers.
First are the arrows for nickels and pushing the dispense button.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa02.pdf}
\end{center}
After receiving $30$~cents and getting another nickel,
this machine does something not very sensible: it stays in $q_{30}$.
In practice we would have further states to keep track of
overages so that we could give change but here we ignore that.
Next comes the arrows for dimes
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa03.pdf}
\end{center}
and for quarters.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa04.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
This machine, when started in state~$q_0$ and fed bit strings,
will keep track of the remainder modulo~$4$ of the number of
\str{1}'s.
\begin{center}    
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa06.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}\footnotetext[1]{%
  US coin denominations are: $1$~cent coins that are not used here,
  nickles are $5$~cents, dimes are $10$~cents, and quarters are $25$~cents.}

%<*def:FSM>
\begin{definition} \label{def:FiniteStateMachine}
A \definend{Finite State machine},
or
\definend{Finite State automata}, is composed of five things
$\FSM=\sequence{Q,q_{\text{start}},F,\Sigma,\Delta}$.
They are a finite \definend{set of states}~$Q$,
one of which is the \definend{start state}~$q_{\text{start}}$,
a subset $F\subseteq Q$ of \definend{accepting states} or
\definend{final states},
a finite \definend{input alphabet} set~$\Sigma$,
and a \definend{next-state function}
or \citetext{\definend{transition function} $\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{Q}$}{%
  Some authors allow the transition function to be partial.
  That is, some authors allow that for some state-symbol pairs
  there is no next state.
  This choice by an author is a matter of convenience, as for any such
  machine you can create a state $q_{\text{error}}$ that is not an 
  accepting state and that transitions only to itself, 
  and send all such pairs there.
  This transition function is total, and the new machine has the same
  collection of accepted strings as the old.}.
\end{definition}
%</def:FSM>

This may not immediately appear to be like to the
definition of a Turing Machine, \cref{def:TuringMachine} from Chapter~One
but with the write commands omitted.
A lot of that is because here we don't need to expand on the terms
`alphabet' or `transition function', since we already have seen them.
All of the other differences between the two follow from the fact that that
Finite State machines cannot write.
For one thing, because Finite State machines cannot write they don't need
to move the tape for scratch work,
so we've dropped the tape action symbols \str{L} and~\str{R}.

At the heart of each of the two definitions is
the transition function~$\Delta$.
It makes machines move step-by-step, from configuration to configuration,
in response to the input.

To work a Finite State machine, put input on the tape and press the Start
button.
The machine starts consuming the input, at each step reading in the next
tape character.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{asy/share/machine02.pdf}
\end{center}
Consequently there is no \HP{} for Finite State machines\Dash they always halt
after a number of steps equal to the length of the input.

The other difference between Finite State machines and Turing machines
is the presence of the accepting states.
Consider, in the vending machine of \cref{ex:VendingMachine},
the state $q_{30}$.
It is a success state or goal state, meaning
that the machine has seen in the input what it is looking for.
The same goes for \cref{ex:Turnstile}'s turnstile state~$q_{\text{ready}}$
and \cref{ex:PowerSwitch}'s power switch state~$q_{\text{on}}$.
While we can design a Turing Machine to indicate a choice by arranging so
that for each input
the machine will halt and the only thing on the tape will be either a \str{1}
or \str{0},
a Finite State machine.
gives a decision by ending in one of these designated states.
Above we've pictured that the accepting states are connected to a red light
so that we know when a computation succeeds.
In the transition graphs below we denote the final states with double circles
and in the transition function tables we mark them with
a `\acceptingstatemark'.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}
This machine accepts a string if and only if it
contains at least two \str{0}'s as well as an even number of \str{1}'s.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa11.pdf}}               
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{0}    &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_3$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_4$    \\
    \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_2$ &$q_5$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_4$ &$q_0$    \\
    $q_4$ &$q_5$ &$q_1$    \\
    $q_5$ &$q_5$ &$q_2$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
This machine illustrates the key to designing Finite State machines:~each
state means something.
For instance, $q_4$ means ``so far I have seen one~$0$ and an
odd number of $1$'s.''
And $q_5$ means ``so far I
have seen two \str{0}'s but an odd number of \str{1}'s.''
The way the machine is drawn brings out this principle.
The first row has states that have so far seen an even number of
\str{1}'s, while the second row's states have seen an odd number.
The first column holds states have seen no \str{0}'s,
the second column holds states have seen one,
and the third column has states that have seen two \str{0}'s.
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSAValidIntegers}
%<*ex:FSAValidIntegers>
This machine
accepts strings that are valid as decimal representations of integers.
Thus, it accepts `\str{21}' and `\str{-707}' but does not accept
`\str{501-}'.
Both the transition graph and the table group some inputs together when
they result in the same action.
For instance, when in state~$q_0$ this machine does the same thing
whether the input is \str{+} or \str{-}, namely it passes into
$q_1$.
%</ex:FSAValidIntegers>
The table's $\acceptingstatemark$ next to $q_2$ marks
it as an accepting state.
\begin{center}  \small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa07.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{+}, \str{-} &\str{0}, \ldots{} \str{9} &\tabulartext{else}   \\
    \cline{2-4}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    $q_1$ &$e$   &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    \acceptingstate $q_2$ &$e$   &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    $e$   &$e$   &$e$   &$e$   \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
  Any bad input character sends the machine to the error state~$e$,
  from which the machine never returns.
\end{example}

\Cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs} exlicitly states its alphabet because in the transition
table the columns are labelled \str{0} and~\str{1}.
But often our Finite State machine descriptions assume that a
reader will infer the alphabet from the context.
For instance, the prior example just says `else'. 
In practice we would take the alphabet to be the
set of characters that someone could conceivably enter, 
including letters such as \str{a} and~\str{A} or characters
characters such as exclamation point or open parenthesis.
So a modern standard software design would be to use 
all of \citetext{Unicode}{While in the early days of computers
  characters could be encoded with standards such as ASCII, which includes only
  upper and lower case unaccented letters, digits,
  a few punctuation marks, and a few
  control characters,
  today's global interconnected world needs more.
  The Unicode standard assigns a unique number called a code point to
  every character in every language (to a fair approximation).
  See \autocite{wiki:Unicode}}.
But often for us that detail is not relevant
to the point of the example, so we will pass over it without comment.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}
This machine accepts strings that are members of the set
$\set{\str{jpg},\str{pdf},\str{png}}$ of filename extensions.
Notice that it has more than one final state.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa09.pdf}}
\end{center}
That drawing omits many edges, the ones involving the error state~$e$.
For instance, from state~$q_0$ any input character other than \str{j}
or~\str{p} is an error.
The drawing below shows the extra edges for the top branch; putting in all the
edges would make a mess.
(Cases such as this are where the transition table is better
than the graph picture.
But most of our machines are small and have only a few states and edges
so we typically like the picture.)
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa10.pdf}}
\end{center}
This example illustrates that  
for any finite language there is a Finite State machine that accepts
a string if and only if it is a member of the language.
The idea is to put the strings in alphabetical order, and for ones that have
common prefixes have the machine step through the shared parts together,
as here with \str{pdf} and \str{png}.
\Cref{exer:FiniteLanguageAcceptedBySomeFSM} asks for a proof.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:FSAMultOfThree}
Although they have no scratch memory, Finite State machines can
accomplish useful work.
This machine accepts strings representing a natural number
that is a multiple of three, such as \str{15} and~\str{5013}.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa12.pdf}}
\end{center}
Because $q_0$ is an accepting state, this machine accepts the empty string.
\Cref{exer:FSAModifyMultOFThreeMachine} asks for a modification of
this machine to accept only non-empty strings.
\end{example}

After the definition of Turing Machine we gave a formal
description of the action of such a machine,
on \cpageref{text:DescriptionOfTMAction}.
We can do the same for a Finite State machine.

%<*discussion:FSMConfiguration>
A \definend{configuration} of a Finite State machine $\FSM$ is 
a pair $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,\tau}$, where
$q$ is a state and $\tau$ is a (possibly empty) string of elements 
from the tape alphabet~$\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$.
In the \definend{initial configuration}
the state is the initial state, so ~$\mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau}$,
and $\tau$ is the \definend{input}.

The action of the machine is to transition   
from one configuration to another.
We refer to its configuration after the $s$-th transition as
its configuration at \definend{step}~$s$.
Its configuration at step~$0$ is its initial configuration.

We must describe a transition.
Assume that at step~$i$ the machine $\FSM$ is in the configuration
$\mathcal{C}_i=\sequence{q,\tau_i}$ and that
$\tau_i$ is not the empty string.
In this case, pop the leading input character and call it $c$.
That is,
$c=\tau_i[0]$ and $\tau_{i+1}=\sequence{\tau_i[1],\ldots \tau_i[\,\lh{\tau_i}-1\,]}$.
The machine's next state is $\hat{q}=\Delta(q,c)$ and its next
configuration is $\mathcal{C}_{i+1}=\sequence{\hat{q},\tau_{i+1}}$.

The other case again has that at step~$i$ the machine $\FSM$ is in the
configuration $\mathcal{C}_i=\sequence{q,\tau_i}$, but 
$\tau_i$ is the empty string.
This is then the \definend{halting configuration}
$\mathcal{C}_i=\mathcal{C}_h$,
and if the ending state
is a final state $q\in F$
then the machine \definend{accepts} the input~$\tau$,
otherwise the machine \definend{rejects}~$\tau$.

If two configurations are related by being separated by a single transition
then we write
$\mathcal{C}_i \yields \mathcal{C}_{i+1}$ 
(read aloud as ``yields in one step'').
A \definend{computation} is a sequence 
$
  \mathcal{C}_0 \yields \mathcal{C}_1 \yields \mathcal{C}_2
   \yields \cdots\, \mathcal{C}_h
$
where $\mathcal{C}_o$ is the initial configuration.
Abbreviate such a sequence
with~$\kleenestar{\yields}$ 
(read as ``yields eventually,'' or simply ``yields'').
Thus we could write
$\mathcal{C}_0\mathrel{\kleenestar{\yields}}\mathcal{C}_h$. 
%</discussion:FSMConfiguration>

\begin{example}
The multiple of three machine of the prior example gives this computation.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{5013}}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,013}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,13}
  \yields\sequence{q_0,3}
  \yields\sequence{q_0,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
Since $q_0$ is an accepting state, the machine accepts \str{5013}.
\end{example}

%<*def:FSMLanguage>
\begin{definition}
For a Finite State machine~$\FSM$, the
\definend{language accepted by that machine~$\lang(\FSM)$} is the
set of strings that it accepts.
\end{definition}
%</def:FSMLanguage>

\begin{example}
Finite State machines are easy to translate to code.
Here is a Scheme version of the multiple of three machine.\footnotemark[1]  
\lstinputlisting{automata/scheme/mod3fsm.scm}

\end{example}\footnotetext[1]{%
  One of the great things about the Scheme programming languages is that,
  because the last thing called in \codeinline{multiple-of-three-fsm-helper}
  is itself, the compiler converts the recursion to iteration.
  So we get the expressiveness of recursion with the space conservation
  of iteration.}

\begin{example}
This is a simplified version of 
\citetext{how phone numbers used to be handled in North America}{%
  See the description of the North America Numbering Plan
  \autocite{wiki:NANumbering}.}.
Consider \str{1-802-555-0101}.
The \str{1} takes us along the second line
so the call leaves the local exchange office.
The \str{0} between the \str{8} and the~\str{2} determines that the call
is to a different area code.
Finally the machine processes the local exchange number of \str{555},
and the line number of \str{0101}, and makes the connection.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa13.pdf}
\end{center}
Today's picture is much more complicated.
For one thing, area codes no longer are required to
have a middle digit of \str{0} or~\str{1}.
This additional complication is possible because instead of
\citetext{switching with physical devices}{%
  The devices to do the switching were invented in 1889 by an undertaker whose
  competiter's wife was the local telephone operator and routed calls to
  her husband's business.
  \autocite{wiki:Strowger}},
we now do it in software.
\end{example}

% We close with a definition that emphasizes the role played
% by determinism.

%<*def:ExtendedTransitionFcn>
\begin{definition} \label{def:ExtendedTransitionFcn}
  For any Finite State machine
  with transition
  function $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{Q}$,
  the \definend{extended transition function} 
  $\map{\extended{\Delta}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{Q}$
  gives the state that the machine will end in after starting in the
  start state and consuming the given string.
\end{definition}
%</def:ExtendedTransitionFcn>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:ExtendedTransFcn}
  The extended transition function $\extended{\Delta}$ of this machine
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa15.pdf}}
    \hspace{2em}
    \begin{transitionfunction}
             &\str{a}  &\str{b}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate
      $q_1$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
      $q_2$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
  extends $\Delta$ in that it repeats the first
  row of $\Delta$'s table.
  \begin{equation*}
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{a})=q_1
    \qquad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{b})=q_0
  \end{equation*}
  (We are disregarding that
  that $\Delta$ inputs characters
  while $\extended{\Delta}$ inputs strings of characters,
  so that in the line above `\str{a}' and `\str{b}' are length~one strings.)

  Here is $\extended{\Delta}$'s action on the length~two strings.
  \begin{equation*}
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{aa})=q_1
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{ab})=q_2
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{ba})=q_1
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{bb})=q_0
  \end{equation*}
\end{example}

The definition of the extended transition function brings us back to thinking
about determinism.
That's because $\extended{\Delta}$ would not be well-defined
without determinism; $\Delta$ has one and only one next state
for all input configurations and so, by induction, for all input strings
$\extended{\Delta}$ has one and only one output ending state.

Finally, note the similarity between $\extended{\Delta}$
and $\phi_e$, the function computed by the Turing machine $\TM_e$.
Both take as input the contents of their machine's start tape,
and both give as output their machine's result.

\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
True or false: a Finite State machine's language must be
finite.
\begin{answer}
  False.
  An example is that \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers}'s machine accepts the
  set containing each of these: \str{0}, \str{1}, \str{2}, \ldots
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What happens when the input to the extended transition function
is the empty string?
\begin{answer}
It outputs the length~one string containing the start state,
$\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=q_0$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce the transition function table for \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}.
\begin{answer}
\begin{center}  \small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa11.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{0}     &\str{1}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_3$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_4$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_2$   &$q_5$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_4$ &$q_0$    \\
    $q_4$ &$q_5$ &$q_1$    \\
    $q_5$ &$q_5$ &$q_2$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Rebut ``no Finite State machine can accept the language
$\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\suchthat n\in\N}$
because $n$ is infinite.''
\begin{answer}
First, ``$n$ is infinite'' is wrong.
That set is a language $\set{\str{b}, \str{ab}, \str{a}^2\str{b}, \ldots}$
and in each string from that language the number of \str{a}'s is finite.

Second, this Finite State machine accepts the language.
\begin{center}  \small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa31.pdf}}
\end{center}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:FSAModifyMultOFThreeMachine}
Modify the machine of \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} 
so that it accepts only non-empty strings.
\begin{answer}
\begin{center}  \small
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa22.pdf}}
\end{center}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % Models of Computation, Taylor p 509
For the machines of \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers}, \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs},
\cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}, and \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree},
answer these.
\begin{exparts*}
\item What are the accepting states?  
\item Does it accept the empty string~$\emptystring$?
\item What is the shortest string that it accepts?
\item Is the language of accepted strings infinite?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
For~\cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers} it is $q_2$.
For \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs} it is $q_2$.
For \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions} they are $q_3$, $q_6$, and $q_8$.
For \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} it is $q_0$.
\item
Only \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} accepts the empty string~$\emptystring$ because 
only in this machine is $q_0$ an accepting state.
\item
For \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers} it is any single-digit length one string,
such as \str{1}.
For \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs} it is the length two string~\str{00}.
For \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions} any of the length three strings
\str{jpg}, \str{png}, and~\str{pdf}.
For \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} it is the empty string~$\emptystring$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a 
transition graph and table
for a Finite State machine accepting each language.
Use $\Sigma=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$. 
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has at least two \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has exactly two \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has less than three \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has at least one \str{a} followed by at least one \str{b}}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
  \quad\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa23.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_2$   &$q_2$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\item
  \quad\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa24.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_3$   &$q_2$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_3$   &$q_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\item
  \quad\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa25.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
 \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_3$   &$q_2$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_3$   &$q_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\item
  \quad\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa26.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_1$ &$q_2$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_3$   &$q_2$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
This grammar   
\begin{grammar}
\ntrm{real} \produces{} \ntrm{posreal} \syntaxor \trm{+} \ntrm{posreal} \syntaxor \trm{-} \ntrm{posreal}

\ntrm{posreal} \produces{} \ntrm{natural} \syntaxor \ntrm{natural} \trm{.} \syntaxor \ntrm{natural} \trm{.} \ntrm{natural}

\ntrm{natural} \produces{} \ntrm{digit} \syntaxor \ntrm{digit} \ntrm{natural}

\ntrm{digit} \produces{} \trm{0} \syntaxor \ldots{}  \trm{9}
\end{grammar}
gives rise to a language.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Give five strings that are in the language and five that are not.
\item Is the string \str{.12} in the language?
\item Briefly describe the language.
\item Give a Finite State machine that accepts the language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Five that are: \str{+1}, \str{123}, \str{-123.45}, \str{-0.0},
\str{0}.
Five that are not: \str{+}, \str{+-}, \str{..}, \str{12.3.4}, \str{2+3}
\item No, before the period must come at least one digit
\item It is the set of representations of real numbers with integer part,
and decimal part.
\item \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa41.pdf}}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce a Finite State machine to accept each language.
\begin{exparts}
\item   
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}
    \suchthat \text{every \str{1} in $\sigma$ is both preceeded and followed by a \str{0}}}$
\item   
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ is the binary representation of a number divisible by $4$}}$
\item 
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ is the decimal representation of a number that is even}}$
\item 
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a number divisible by $100$ in base ten}}$
\item Describe a machine for this language.
  You need not give the full graph or table.
  \begin{equation*}    
   \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
     \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a multiple of $4$ in base ten}}
  \end{equation*} 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa42.pdf}}
\item \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa43.pdf}}
\item \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa44.pdf}}
\item \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa45.pdf}}
\item A number is divisible by four if and only if its representation ends in
\str{00}, \str{04}, \ldots \str{96}, or the single-digit cases where the
representation is \str{0}, \str{4},
or~\str{8}.
That's $28$ cases.
The picture is too cluttered to be deciphered but this is a finite language
and for any finite language there is a Finite State machines that
accepts that langugae.
We shall see an alternative approach that makes a clearer
picture in the section on nondeterminism.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  The definition of the extended transition function,
  \cref{def:ExtendedTransitionFcn},
  is conceptual, with some details held back.
  Here is a constructive definition, one that you could easily translate to
  write a computer program.
  On the empty input string,
  $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=q_0$.
  If the input string~$\sigma$ is not empty then
  peel off the final character, $\sigma=\sigma_0\concat x$ for $x\in\Sigma$,
  and let $\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$ be
  $\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0),x)$.
  Now apply this to the machine in \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Use the definition to find $\extended{\Delta}(\str{0})$
    and $\extended{\Delta}(\str{1})$.
  \item Use the definition to find $\extended{\Delta}$'s output on inputs
    \str{00}, \str{01}, \str{10}, and \str{11}.
  \item Find its action on all length~three strings.
  \end{exparts}
  \begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item If the input string is $\sigma=\str{a}$ then peel off the final
    character with
    $\sigma=\sigma_0\concat\str{0}$ and the definition gives this.
    \begin{equation*}
      \extended{\Delta}(\str{0})
      =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring),\str{0})
      =\Delta(q_0,\str{0})
      =q_1
    \end{equation*}
    (Note that in the left-most expression \str{0} is a length one string,
    while in the rest of the above equation \str{0} is a character.)
    Similarly, $\extended{\Delta}(\str{0})=q_3$
  \item For $\sigma=\str{00}$ use the prior item's computation of
    $\extended{\Delta}(\str{0})$.
    \begin{equation*}
      \extended{\Delta}(\str{00})
      =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\str{0}),\str{0})
      =\Delta(q_1,\str{0})
      =q_2
    \end{equation*}
    The other three are similar.
    \begin{equation*}
      \extended{\Delta}(\str{01})=q_4
      \quad\extended{\Delta}(\str{10})=q_4
      \quad\extended{\Delta}(\str{11})=q_0
    \end{equation*}
  \item
    For each of these use the prior item's computation.
    Here is the first.
    \begin{equation*}
      \extended{\Delta}(\str{000})
      =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\str{00}),\str{0})
      =\Delta(q_2,\str{0})
      =q_2
    \end{equation*}
    The others are computed in the same way.
    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{*{8}{c}}
      $\extended{\Delta}(\str{000})$
      &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{001})$
      &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{010})$
      &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{011})$
      &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{100})$
      &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{101})$
      &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{110})$
      &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{111})$  \\ \hline
      $q_2$
      &$q_5$
      &$q_5$
      &$q_1$
      &$q_5$
      &$q_1$
      &$q_1$
      &$q_3$
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}
  \end{exparts}
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $\Sigma=\B$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
List all of the different
Finite State machines over $\Sigma$ with $Q=\set{q_0}$.
(Ignore whether a state is final or not; we will do that below.)

\item
List all the the ones with $Q=\set{q_0,q_1}$.

\item
How many machines are there with $n$ states?

\item
What if we distinguish between machines with different sets of final states?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
There is one such machine, this one.
\begin{center}
\begin{transitionfunction}
        &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$
\end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}

\item
This list has $16$ machines.
\begin{center}
\begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_0]
        &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
  $q_1$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
\end{transitionfunction}
\hspace{1em}
\begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_1]
        &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
  $q_1$ &$q_0$    &$q_1$    \\
\end{transitionfunction}
\hspace{1em}
\begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_2]
        &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
  $q_1$ &$q_1$    &$q_0$    \\
\end{transitionfunction}
\hspace{2em}\ldots\hspace{2em}
\begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_{15}]
        &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $q_0$ &$q_1$    &$q_1$    \\
  $q_1$ &$q_1$    &$q_1$    \\
\end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}

\item
Fix $n$ states $Q=\set{q_0,\ldots q_{n-1}}$.
Each line of the transition table has two slots so the number of
distinct lines is $n\cdot n=n^2$.
An overall table chooses $n$~such lines.
There are $n^2\cdot n^2 \cdots n^2=n^{2n}$ many ways to do that.

\item
For each of the $n$~states, either it is final or it is not.
The additional factor of $2^n$ makes the total number
$2^nn^{2n}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\textit{Propositiones ad acuendos iuvenes} (problems to sharpen
the young) is the oldest collection
of mathematical problems written in Latin.
It teaches logical thinking and problem solving and was 
written by Alcuin of York (735--804),
head of the Frankish court school at Aachen and a royal advisor
to Charlemagne.
One problem, titled
\textit{Propositio de lupo et capra etfasciculo cauli}, is famous.
\textit{A man had to transport to the far side of a river
\citetext{a wolf, a goat, and a bundle of cabbages}{%  Annie Raymond
  This translation is from A~Raymond, from the University of Washington.}.
The only boat he could find was one that could
carry only two of them. For that reason, he sought a plan which would
enable them all to get to the far side unhurt. Let him, who is able, say
how it could be possible to transport them safely.}
Readers from that time would have understood that a wolf cannot be left
alone with a goat nor can a goat be left alone with cabbages.
Construct the relevant Finite State machine and use it to solve the problem.
\begin{answer}
We can have the man, the wolf, the goat, and the cabbage,
on the near or far side.
The allowed transitions are: man crossses with wolf,
man crosses with goat, man crosses with cabbage, and man crosses by himself.
Here are the states; some of them lead to an error state, as readers of the
$700$'s would have understood.

In this Finite State machine the start state is $s_0$ and the only
accepting state is~$s_{15}$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cc|cccc}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{state}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{meaning}}
                          &\str{with wolf}
                          &\str{with goat}
                          &\str{with cabbage}
                          &\str{with nothing}  \\     
   $s_0$  &n:~mwgc, f:~--- &$s_{12}$ &$s_{10}$  &$s_9$    &$s_8$      \\
   $s_1$  &n:~mwg, f:~c    &$s_{13}$ &$s_{11}$  &$e$      &$s_9$   \\
   $s_2$  &n:~mwc, f:~g    &$s_{14}$ &$e$       &$s_{11}$ &$s_{10}$     \\
   $s_3$  &n:~mw, f:~gc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
   $s_4$  &n:~mgc, f:~w    &$e$      &$s_{14}$  &$s_{13}$ &$s_{12}$      \\
   $s_5$  &n:~mg, f:~wc    &$e$      &$s_{15}$  &$e$      &$s_{13}$      \\
   $s_6$  &n:~mc, f:~wg    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$        \\
   $s_7$  &n:~m, f:~wgc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$       \\
   $s_8$  &n:~wgc, f:~m--  &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
   $s_9$  &n:~wg, f:~mc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
   $s_{10}$  &n:~wc, f:~mg &$e$      &$s_0$     &$e$     &$s_2$       \\
   $s_{11}$  &n:~w, f:~mgc &$e$      &$s_1$     &$s_{10}$ &$s_3$        \\
   $s_{12}$  &n:~gc, f:~mw &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$        \\
   $s_{13}$  &n:~g, f:~mwc &$s_1$    &$e$       &$s_4$    &$s_5$         \\
   $s_{14}$  &n:~c, f:~mwg &$s_2$    &$s_4$     &$e$      &$s_6$         \\
   $s_{15}$  &n:~---, f:~mwgc  &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$  \\
   $e$    &error           &$e$     &$e$       &$e$      &$e$   \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Here is the solution in words:
\textit{The man first brings the goat to the far side and leaves it there.
He goes back and brings the wolf with him, leaving it on the far side,
and brings back the goat.
He leaves the goat on the initial side, takes the cabbage and brings it
to the far side.
Finally, he goes back to the original shore, and takes the goat to bring
it to the far side.}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider a variant on a Finite State machine, where the set of input
strings is bounded.
\begin{exparts}
\item
In Rock-Paper-Scissors,
two players each simultaneously
produce one of the three \str{R}, \str{P}, or~\str{S}.  
A player producing \str{R} beats a player producing~\str{S},
and similarly \str{S} beats~\str{P}, and \str{P} beats~\str{R}
(if they produce the same then it is a do-over).
Encode a game instance with the two-character string
$\sigma=\sequence{\text{player one's play, player two's play}}$.
Make a machine that recognizes a win for player one.
\item
Make a machine that accepts a Tic-Tac-Toe game that is a win for
the \str{X}'s.
A board has nine squares so encode a game instance with length nine strings.
\end{exparts}
Both the examples here illustrate particularly well how the machines
use their states as a kind of memory\Dash to remember, in a way,
what has happened in the compuation so far.
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
There are three ways for the first player to win.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa36.pdf}}
\end{center}
\item
Encode the ending position as a length nine string.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|c|c}
  $a_{0,0}$ &$a_{0,1}$ &$a_{0,2}$  \\ \hline
  $a_{1,0}$ &$a_{1,1}$ &$a_{1,2}$  \\ \hline
  $a_{2,0}$ &$a_{2,1}$ &$a_{2,2}$  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\hspace{1em}$\Longrightarrow$\hspace{1em}$a_{0,0}a_{1,0}\ldots a_{2,2}$
\qquad $a_{i,j}\in\Sigma=\set{\str{X},\str{O},\str{B}}$
\end{center}
Here, \str{B} means that the square is blank, because the game ended before
that square was filled.
We are only interested in wins for player one so we only care about which
squares are occupied by \str{X}'s.
Thus, we take the alphabet to be $\hat{\Sigma}=\set{\str{X},\str{E}}$,
where \str{E} means `else'. 
There are eight ways for \str{X} to win.
\begin{align*}
&\begin{array}{c|c|c}
  \str{X} &\str{X} &\str{X} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\  
\end{array},\hspace{1em}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{X} &\str{X} &\str{X} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\  
\end{array},\hspace{1em}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{X} &\str{X} &\str{X} \\  
  \end{array},                                \\
&\quad
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
  \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\  
\end{array},\hspace{1em}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
  \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\  
\end{array},\hspace{1em}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\  
\end{array},                              \\  
&\quad
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
  \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\  
\end{array},\hspace{1em}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
  \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\ \hline
  \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\ \hline
  \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\  
\end{array}
\end{align*}
The machine takes the shape of a binary tree of depth nine.
It has $2^9$ branches and $2^{10}$ total state nodes, too many to
show here so this picture just shows ellipses.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa49.pdf}}
\end{center}
Put double-circle states for the terminal node in each of the eight
branches described above, such as
\str{XXXEEEEEE}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Can a Finite State machine have no states?
Does the definition allow $Q=\emptyset$?  
\begin{answer}
No, a Finite State machine cannot have $Q=\emptyset$.
The definition requires a start state.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that any language accepted by a Finite State machine is
accepted by some Turing machine.
(There are lots of ways to define `accepted by a Turing machine'.
One is that the Turing machine
reads the input and halts with the head under the only symbol
on the tape, a \str{1}.)
\begin{answer}
Fix some Finite State machine~$\FSM$.
We shall  outline a computer program that acts as this machine.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine equivalent.

The program has as a constant the Finite State machine's
transition function~$\Delta$,
perhaps simply storing the transition table as an array.
The program uses this to simulate the Finite State machine.
As input this program takes the input of the Finite State machine.

This simulation halts because a Finite State machine must halt.
When it does so, the Turing machine erases the tape.
If the simulation ended in an accepting state then
the Turing machine writes a \str{1}, otherwise it writes a~\str{0}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix a Finite State machine $\FSM$, its set of accepting states~$F$,
and the associated language~$\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\Sigma}$
of accepted strings.
If we flip every accepting state to non-accepting and every non-accepting
state to accepting, what happens to the language?
\begin{answer}
If we take the new set of final states to be $\hat{F}=Q-F$ then the
machine will accept the complementary language
$\hat{\lang}=\kleenestar{\Sigma}-\lang$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:FiniteLanguageAcceptedBySomeFSM}
Show, as suggested by
\cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}, that for any finite language,
there is a Finite State machine that accepts a string if and only if it
is a member of that language.
\begin{answer}
This is based on \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}.

We will prove this by induction.
If the language is empty then we use any
Finite State machine with the right alphabet and no accepting states.
If the language has one string~$\sigma$ then we write a Finite State machine
whose state transitions track the characters of~$\sigma$ as does the top line
in the example.
That is, we have this.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa46.pdf}
\end{center}
As the diagram shows, the last state in that sequence is an accepting state.
Also as shown, all transitions for characters not in the sequence go to $e$.
(If $\sigma=\emptystring$ then there are two states,
with the initial state~$q_0$ an accepting
state and all transitions going to the error state~$e$ that is not an accepting
state.)

For the inductive case,
assume that there is a machine for any language of size $n=1$, \ldots $n=k$
and consider a language~$\lang$ with $k+1$-many strings.
Order the alphabet $\Sigma$; in the example
we use the grade school alphabetical ordering.
This character ordering gives rise to an ordering of the strings,
called lexicographic ordering.
List $\lang$'s strings in this order:
$\sigma_0$, \ldots $\sigma_k$, $\sigma_{k+1}$.
The induction assumption gives a machine for the language
consisting of the strings $\sigma_0$, \ldots $\sigma_k$.
The example shows what to do next; it has the two final strings
\str{png} and \str{pdf} and the machine splits where the two strings split,
after the~\str{p}.
Following that model, 
find the longest common substring for $\sigma_k$ and~$\sigma_{k+1}$,
so that $\sigma_k=\alpha\concat\beta$, and $\sigma_{k+1}=\alpha\concat\gamma$,
and the initial character of $\beta$ is not equal to the initial character
of $\gamma$.
(It could be that $\beta=\emptystring$.
It could also be that $\alpha=\emptystring$.)
Split the machine after it traverses the common string, as in the example.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa47.pdf}
\end{center}
This figure omits all the transitions involving~$e$, to be more readable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Show that the set of Finite State machines is infinite.
  Is it countable?
  \hint~you must decide what the question means.
  For instance, what
  is the alphabet?
\begin{answer}
  We will show that for any alphabet there are infinitely many
  different Finite State machines.
  Fix any $\Sigma$ and suppose $\str{s}\in\Sigma$.
  The machines below differ in the number of states,
  and in that they accept different languages.
  (If $\Sigma$ contains characters other than~\str{s}
  then use the second column.)
  \begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &other \\ \cline{2-2}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_0$ &q_0
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}  
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &other \\ \cline{2-2}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$ \\
    $q_1$ &$q_0$ &$q_0$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}  
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &other  \\ \cline{2-2}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$  \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_0$  \\
    $q_2$ &$q_0$ &$q_0$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}\ldots  
  \end{center}
  That shows, under a reasonable interpretation of what it means for
  machines to differ, that there are at least countably many Finite State
  machines.
  (The technical wording is that these machines are not `isomprphic'.)

  The argument that there are no more than countably many machines also
  requires an interpretation.
  We assume that the set of possible states is
  $\set{q_0,q_1,\ldots}$ so as to not get uncountably many
  machines by virtue of there being uncountably many possible states.
  With that, the argument that we can count the
  Finite State machines is straightforward since for any collection of
  states $q_0,\ldots q_k$ there are finitely many transition tables.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Variants: Moore machine, Mealy machine
% \end{exercise}
\end{exercises}




% ==================================================
\section{Nondeterminism}
Turing machines, and also Finite State machines, have the property that
the next state is completely determined by the current state and current
character.
Once you lay out an initial tape and push Start then
you just walk through the steps like, well, like an automaton.
We now consider machines that are nondeterministic, 
where from any configuration the machine could move to more than
one next state, or to just one, or even to no state at all.


\subsection{Motivation}
Suppose that you have a grammar
with some rules and start symbol~$S$.
You want to know if the string~$\sigma$ has a derivation.
The challenge to these problems is that
the derivation can potentially go more than one way.
For instance, if the first rule is
\mbox{S\produces{}\trm{a}S\syntaxor\trm{b}A} then
from S you can do two different things;
which one will work? 

In the derivation exercises we used intuition to
spot that one way or another leads to an answer.
If you wrote a program for it then you might instead try every
possible derivation.
For instance, you might do a breadth-first traversal of the tree of all
derivations.

Although the breadth-first technique is familiar from experience with
programming classes, don't think that it is natural. 
This problem is most naturally done in parallel, and breadth-first traversal is
a serialization that we do only because it is required by
the machine model that we have, that is,
on the devices that we have in mind when we are programming.

\margingraphic{0.175\textwidth}{automata/pix/yogi.jpg}{\label{fig:yogi}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogi_Berra}{Yogi Berra 1925--2015}}
The American philosopher, and Hall of Fame baseball catcher, Y~Berra
said, ``If you come to a fork in the road, take it.''
That's a natural way to attack this problem:~when you come up against
multiple possibilities,
fork a child for each.
Thus, the routine might begin with the start state~$S$ and for each rule that
could apply it spawns a child process that does apply that rule, deriving a
string one removed from the start.
Then each child finds each rule that could apply to its string and
spawns its own children, each of which now has a string that is two removed from
the start.
Continue until the desired string appears, if it ever does.

The celebrated Travelling Salesman Problem illustrates.
Take a map of the forty eight contiguous US~states
and look at trips that visit every state capitol.
We want to know, say, if there is a cycle of less than $16\,000$ kilometers.
Starting at Montpelier we could
fork forty seven child processes, one for each potential next capital.
The process assigned Albany, for instance, would know that the trip so far is
$126$~kilometers. 
Each child would then fork its own
children, forty six of them.
At the end, if there is
a trip of less than $16\,000$ kilometers then some process knows it.
There will be lots of processes and many of them may have failed
to find a short enough trip, but if even one succeeded then we consider
the overall search a success.

This computation is nondeterministic in that during it
the machine has many process.
In that sense the machine is simultaneously in many different states.

This approach suits an unboundedly-parallel machine, which
can always allocate new children.
Or, a single CPU could simulate it with time-slicing,
which dovetails working with the first process for a few ticks,
then turns to the second, and then back to the first, the second, and then
to the third, etc.
This echos our experience with everyday computers, where
when we are writing an email in one window 
and watching a video in another then the machine seems to be
in multiple states.

This section considers nondeterministic Finite State machines.
(We will do nondeterministic Turing machines in the fifth chapter.)
We will have two ways to think about nondeterminism,
two mental models.\footnote{%
  While they are helpful in learning and thinking about nondeterminism,
  these models are not part of the formal definitions and proofs.}
The first was introduced above.
We can picture that when it is presented with multiple possible next states, 
the machine forks, so it is in all of them simultaneously.
This example illustrates.
% The second way is that the machine simply guesses.
% The first one seems more natural to a person with programming experience,
% so we will illustrate it first.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMInDiscussion}
This machine is nondeterministic because 
leaving $q_0$ are two arrows labelled~\str{0}.
It also has states with a deficit of edges; e.g.,
out of $q_1$ comes no~\str{1} arrow.
\begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm00.pdf}}
\end{center}
Give it input \str{00001}.
As with the unboundedly parallel approach discusses above,
we there are two next states then 
the machine splits in two.
As the animation below shows,
the computation history is a tree.

\begin{animatedfigure}{Steps in the nondeterministic computation.} 
  \animategraphics[poster=last]{3}{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm}{02}{08}
  \label{anim:NondeterministicComputationTree}
\end{animatedfigure}
% \begin{center}
%   \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm08.pdf}
% \end{center}
\end{example}
When we considered the forking approach to string derivations or to
the Travelling Salesman,
we observed that if a solution exists then some child process would find it.
The same happens here; there is a
branch of the comptuation tree that accepts the input string.
Now, there are also branches that are not successful.
One is 
the one at the bottom dies out after step~$2$
because this machine has no next state when the present
state~$q_2$ and the input is~\str{0}.
Another is the branch at the top, which never dies but
also does not accept the input.
However, we don't care about unsuccessful branches.
We only care that there exists a successful one.
So we will define that a nondeterministic machine accepts an input
if there is at least one branch in the computation tree that accepts the
input.

\margingraphic{0.20\textwidth}{automata/pix/flauros.jpg}{\label{fig:Flauros}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_demons_in_the_Ars_Goetia}{Flauros, Duke of Hell, gives true answers of all things past, present, and future}} % https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AIll_dict_infernal_p0294-278_flauros_demon.jpg Louis Le Breton [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
% \margingraphic{0.25\textwidth}{automata/demon.jpg}{\label{fig:Demon}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Nightmare}{\textit{The Nightmare} H~Fuseli, 1781}} % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/56/John_Henry_Fuseli_-_The_Nightmare.JPG  Henry Fuseli [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons  (https://www.atlasobscura.com/articles/original-nightmare-demon-suffocation-night-terror)
The above machine accepts a string if it ends in two~\str{0}'s and a~\str{1}.
When we feed it the input \str{00001} the problem the machine faces is
when it should stop going around $q_0$'s loop and start to the right.
Our definition has the machine accepting this input
so the machine has solved this problem\Dash viewed from the outside,
we could, perhaps somewhat fancifully, say that the
machine has correctly guessed.

This is our second mental model for nondeterminism.
We will imagine programming by calling a function, some 
\citetext{$\operatorname{amb}(S,R_0,R_1\ldots R_{n-1})$}{%
  The name \textit{amb}
  abbreviates `ambiguous function'.
  Here is a small example.
  Essentially \str{Amb(x,y,z)} splits the computation
  into three possible futures:
  a future in which the value \str{x} is yielded,
  a future in which the value \str{y} is yielded,
  and a future in which the value \str{z} is yielded.
  The future which leads to a successful subsequent computation is chosen.
  The other ``parallel universes'' somehow go away.
  (Amb called with no arguments fails.)
% \begin{lstlistings}
% (let ((x (amb 1 2 3))
%       (y (amb 5 4 3)))
%   (amb (= (* x y) 8)
%   (println x y)))
% \end{lstlistings}
  The output is $2$~$4$ because \str{Amb(1,2,3)} correctly chooses the future
  in which \str{x} has value $2$, \str{Amb(7,6,4,5)} chooses $4$,
  and consequently
  \str{Amb(x*y = 8)} produces a success.
  
  These were described by John McCarthy in \autocite{McCarthy}.
  ``Ambiguous functions are not really functions.
  For each prescription of values
  to the arguments the ambiguous function has a collection of possible values.
  An example of an ambiguous function is
  $\operatorname{less}(n)$ defined for all positive integer
  values of $n$.
  Every non-negative integer less than $n$ is a possible value of
  $\operatorname{less}(n)$.
  First we define a basic ambiguity operator $\operatorname{amb}(x, y)$
  whose possible
  values are $x$ and~$y$ when both are defined: otherwise, whichever is defined.
  Now we can define $\operatorname{less}(n)$ by
  $\operatorname{less}(n) = \operatorname{amb}(n-1, \operatorname{less}(n-1))$.''},
and having the computer somehow guess a successful search sequence.

Saying that the machine is guessing is jarring since  
\citetext{it does not fit our intuition of how computers work}{%
  \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Aq_1l316ow8}{``That's not how this works, that's not how any of this works''}}.
Nonetheless, researchers in this area often speak this way.
A perhaps less disconcerting way to put it is to imagine that the machine is
furnished with the answer (``go around twice, then off to the right'') and
only has to check it.
The furnisher is often personified as a demon, a supernatural being,
who somehow knows answers that cannot otherwise be found,
but you are suspicious and must check that the answer is not a trick.
So, under this mental model we say that a nondeterministic computation
accepts the input if there exists a branch of the computation tree
that the machine could check.

We shall describe nondeterminism in both ways, as a
machine being in multiple states at once and as a machine guessing.
To use both paradigms we must reconcile them by showing
that there is a nondeterministic machine accepting
a string under the first model if and only if there is one accepting
that string under the second.
We start by defining Nondeterministic
Finite State machines.
% and showing that they
% accept exactly the same set of languages
% as deterministic ones.




\subsection{Definition}

A nondeterministic Finite State machine
differs from that of a deterministic machine only in that
the next-state function does not output single states,
it outputs sets of states.

%<*def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine>
\begin{definition} \label{def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine}
A \definend{nondeterministic Finite State machine}
$\FSM=\sequence{Q,q_{\text{start}},F,\Sigma,\Delta}$ 
consists of a finite \definend{set of states}~$Q$,
one of which is the \definend{start state}~$q_{\text{start}}$,
a subset $F\subseteq Q$ of \definend{accepting states}
(or \definend{final states}),
a finite \definend{input alphabet} set~$\Sigma$,
and a \definend{next-state function}
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{\powerset (Q)}$.
\end{definition}
%</def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine>

We will use these machines in three ways.
First, we need them to prove Kleene's Theorem,
\cref{th:KleenesTheorem} below.
Second, we also present them because they are useful in practice.
% Often, solving a complex job is much easier with a nondeterministic machine,
% and below we show how to convert such a solution to one using a
% deterministic machine, which is then easy to convert to code.
For some examples of complex jobs that are more easily solved in this way,
see the exercises.
Finally, they are our first encounter with the
concept of nondeterminism, which is critical for the book's final part. 


\begin{example}  \label{ex:NDFMFromDiscussion}
This is \cref{ex:NFSMInDiscussion}'s nondeterministic Finite State machine,
along with its transition function.
\begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm00.pdf}}
  \hspace*{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{0}    &\str{1}             \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$  &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $q_1$  &$\set{q_2}$      &$\set{}$     \\
    $q_2$  &$\set{}$         &$\set{q_3}$  \\
    \acceptingstate{} $q_3$  &$\set{}$     &$\set{}$   \\    
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
\end{example}

%<*def:NFSMAccepted>
\begin{definition}
\label{def:NFSMAccepted}
A nondeterministic Finite State machine \definend{accepts} an input
string if there is some sequence of transitions for that string allowed by
the machine that ends in an accepting state.
The \definend{language of the machine~$\lang(\FSM)$} is the set
of accepted strings.
\end{definition}
%</def:NFSMAccepted>

\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSMSubstringAAOrBB}
The language accepted by this machine
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm09.pdf}
\end{center}
is the set of strings containing the substring \str{aa} or \str{bb}.
For instance, the machine accepts \str{abaaba}, where the 
matching substring starts at index~$2$, because there is a
sequence of allowed transitions ending in an accepting state,
namely this.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{abaaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{baaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{aaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{aba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{ba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{a}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
\end{example}


\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSMRepeatsofAC}
This machine, with $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$,
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm10.pdf}
\end{center}
accepts $\set{(\str{ac})^n\suchthat n\in\N}
  =\set{\emptystring,\str{ac},\str{acac},\,\ldots\,}$.
The symbol~\str{b} isn't attached to any arrow
so it won't play a part in any accepting string.
\end{example}

One advantage of nondeterministic Finite State machines is that
they are easy to write.

\begin{example}
Give a machine that accepts any string in $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$
whose next to last character is \str{a}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm62.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Give a machine that accepts
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma=0\concat\tau\concat 1$ where $\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}$}}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm63.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
This nondeterministic
garage door opener listener waits to hear the remote control send
the signal \str{0101110}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm11.pdf}
\end{center}
That is, it accepts the language
$\set{\sigma\concat\str{0101110}\suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$
of strings with that suffix.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
Note again that something in that example
can be disconcerting.
The listener will end in an accepting 
state if it starts in state~$q_0$ and
hears a string \str{010101110}, with three \str{01}'s.
How does this mechanism guess that it should ignore
the first \str{01} but pay attention to the second?
A person trained in
programming may well react that ``guessing'' is a
not-mechanically-accomplishable task.

In mathematics
we can consider whatever we can precisely define 
but in this book we have so far emphasized
devices that are physically realizable in principle,
so this may seem like a change in direction. 
However, we will show how to convert a nondeterministic Finite State
machine into  deterministic one.
So we can think of the above machine
as an abbreviation for a deterministic one,
which obviates the paradox of guessing, at least for this class of machine.
\end{remark}



\subsection{$\varepsilon$ transitions}
%<*discussion:epsilonTransitions>
Another extension of Finite State machines
allows machines to transition spontaneously, without consuming any input.
The only change in the definition is that the
transition function's signature
goes from $\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{\powerset(Q)}$
to
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times(\Sigma\union\set{\emptystring})}{\powerset(Q)}$
(where $\varepsilon$ is not an element of~$\Sigma$).
The semantics, the associated behavior,
is that the machine may change state spontaneously, 
without any input.
%</discussion:epsilonTransitions>

\begin{example}
This nondeterministic Finite State machine accepts valid integers.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm12.pdf}
\end{center}
The $\varepsilon$-transition  
allows the machine to spontaneously go from
from $q_0$ to~$q_1$.
With this, the machine can accept strings that do not start with a sign.
For instance, if we start this machine in~$q_0$
and give it input~\str{123} then it 
can follow the $\varepsilon$
transition to state~$q_1$, then read the \str{1} and transition
to $q_2$, staying there while processing the \str{2} and \str{3}.
This is a branch of the computation tree accepting the input,
so our definition says that the machine accepts the input.
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMJoinParallel}
  A $\varepsilon$ transition can put two machines together using
  parallel connection.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm14.pdf}
\end{center}
The top nondeterministic machine's language is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{ab}}}$
and the bottom machine's language is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{$\sigma=(\str{ac})^n$ for some $n\in\N$}}$,
where $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$.
These two are unioned to make the language for the entire machine.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
    \text{either $\sigma$ ends in \str{ab} or $\sigma=(\str{ac})^n$ for $n\in\N$}}
\end{equation*}
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSM:JoinSerial}
  An $\varepsilon$ transition can also tie together two machines by
  using a serial connection.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm13.pdf}
\end{center}
The left side is a nondeterministic machine.
If $q_0$ were an accepting state then its language would be
$\set{(\str{aab})^m\suchthat m\in\N}$.
The right side 
accepts strings in $\set{(\str{a}|\str{aba})^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
Overall, the machine accepts strings in the concatenation of those languages
\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal{L}(\FSM)=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma=(\str{aab})^m(\str{a}|\str{aba})^n$ for $m,n\in\N$}}
\end{equation*}
That is, a string is accepted by the overall machine
if first comes some number of \str{aab}'s,
and then comes some number of: either \str{a}'s or \str{aba}'s.
For example, the machine
accepts these two strings; the brackets help
a person's eye pick out the parts.
\begin{equation*}
  \newlength{\jhscratchlength} \setlength{\jhscratchlength}{0.25pt}
  \underbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{aab}}\overbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{aab}}\underbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{aba}}\overbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{aba}}
  \qquad
  \underbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{aab}}\overbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{a}}\underbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{aba}}\overbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{a}}\underbracket[\jhscratchlength]{\str{aba}}
\end{equation*}
% Note that in the second part of a string 
% the \str{a}'s can be interwoven with the \str{aba}'s.
\end{example}


\begin{example} \label{ex:EpsilonTransMakeKleeneStar}
A $\varepsilon$ transition edge can also take a nondeterministic machine and
give it the ``any number of repetitions'' effect.
That is, without that edge this machine's language is
$\set{\str{ab}}$
while adding it makes the lanaguage  
$\set{(\str{ab})^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm15.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

As with nondeterminstic machines above, 
one reason $\varepsilon$ transitions are widely used is that
they often make solving a complex job much easier.
Examples are in the exercises.



\subsection{Equivalence of the machine types}\label{sec:EquivDFSMAndNFSM}
For Finite State machines, nondeterminism does not alter the power of the
machines and so
our first encounter is that it is less a radical innovation and more
of an engineering convenience.

%<*th:dfsmEquivndfsm>
\begin{theorem}
  The set of languages accepted by deterministic Finite State machines
  equals the set of languages accepted by nondeterministic Finite State
  machines.
  This remains true if we allow the nondeterministic machines to
  have $\varepsilon$ transitions.
\end{theorem}
%</th:dfsmEquivndfsm>

To show that the two sets are equal we will argue that they are subsets of
each other.
One direction is easy:
any deterministic machine is a nondeterministic machine.
That is, in a deterministic machine the next-state function outputs
single states.
To make it a nondeterministic machine just convert those single states
into singleton sets.
Thus the assertion that the set of languages accepted by
deterministic machines is a subset of the set accepted by
nondeterministic machines is trivial.

For inclusion the other way
we will demonstrate how to start
with a nondeterministic machine, with $\varepsilon$ transitions, and
construct a deterministic machine that accepts the same language.
We will not give a complete proof, although
certainly one is possible.
But a proof is a notational mess and the examples below
are convincing.

First consider a nondeterministic machine $\FSM_N$, with
next-state function~$\Delta_N$, which has no $\varepsilon$ transitions.
The states $s_i$ of the deterministic machine $\FSM_D$ are
the sets of states of $\FSM_N$, so that $s_i=\set{q_{i_1},q_{i_2}, \,\ldots}$.
The next-state function $\Delta_D$ of $\FSM_D$  combines the
next states associated with each $q_{i_j}$.
\begin{equation*}
  \Delta_D(s_i)=
    \Delta_N(q_{i_1})\cup\Delta_N(q_{i_2})\cup \cdots
  \tag*{($*$)}
\end{equation*}

\begin{example}
Consider this nondeterministic machine $\FSM_N$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm16.pdf}
\end{center}
The deterministic machine $\FSM_D$ that has the
same behavior as $\FSM_N$ is below.

The deterministic $\FSM_D$'s states~$s_i$ are the sets of states of $\FSM_N$.
For an example of computing the next-state function, 
suppose that this machine is in $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$ and is reading~\str{a}.
Apply equation~($*$):
combine the set of next states due to~$q_0$, the set
$\Delta_N(q_0,\str{a})=\set{q_0,q_1}$, with the next states
due to~$q_2$, the set $\Delta_N(q_2,\str{a})=\set{}$.
That gives $\Delta_D(s_5,\str{a})=\set{q_0,q_1}$, which is the state~$s_4$.
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_D]
      &\str{a}   &\str{b}         \\
      \cline{2-3}
    $s_0=\set{}$                           &$s_0$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_1=\set{q_0}$         &$s_4$  &$s_0$  \\
    $s_2=\set{q_1}$                         &$s_0$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_3=\set{q_2}$         &$s_0$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_4=\set{q_0,q_1}$     &$s_4$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$     &$s_4$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_1,q_2}$      &$s_0$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_7=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  &$s_4$  &$s_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
A state of $\FSM_D$ is accepting if any of its elements are accepting
states in $\FSM_N$.
The start state of $\FSM_D$ is $s_1=\set{q_0}$ because
the start state of $\FSM_N$ is~$q_0$.

Besides the notational convenience,
naming the sets of states as $s_i$'s makes clear that
$\FSM_D$ is a deterministic Finite State machine.
Here's a transition graph.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm17.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

If the nondeterministic machine has~$k$-many states then
under this construction the deterministic machine has $2^k$-many states.
Typically many of them can be eliminated.
For instance, in the above machine the state~$s_6$ is unreachable 
since there are no arrows into $s_6$.
The next section covers minimizing the number of states in a machine.

Next we expand the above construction to a nondeterministic machine
with $\varepsilon$ transitions.
We have to follow those transitions.
For instance, suppose that
we are constructing the associated deterministic machine's
next-state function~$\Delta_D$,
that the current configuration is $s_i=\set{q_{i_1},q_{i_2}, \,\ldots}$  and
that the machine is reading \str{a}.
If there is a $\varepsilon$ transition from $q_{i_j}$ to some $q$ then we must
add to equation~($*$) the term $\Delta_N(\set{q},\str{a})$.
Further, we have to iterate:~if there is a
$\varepsilon$ transition leading out of $q$ then
we must handle its endpoint the same way.
Following sequences of $\varepsilon$ transitions is called finding
the $\varepsilon$ closure; see \cref{exer:DefnEpsilonClosure}.

One more way that we must follow the $\varepsilon$~transitions
is that the start state will be all the states of $\FSM_N$ that
are reachable by $\varepsilon$~transitions from $q_0$,
that is, the $\varepsilon$~closure of the set $\set{q_0}$.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMEpsilonClosure}
Consider this nondeterministic machine.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm18.pdf}
\end{center}
Suppose it is in state $q_0$ and is reading \str{a}.
The arrow on the left takes the machine
from $q_0$ to~$q_2$.
Alternatively, starting at $q_0$,
following the $\varepsilon$ transition to~$q_3$,
and then reading the \str{a} gives~$q_3$.
So the machine is next in the set of states $\set{q_0,q_3}$.

This is the full deterministic machine. 
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_D]
      &\str{a}   &\str{b}         \\
      \cline{2-3}
    $s_0=\set{}$                                 &$s_0$     &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_1=\set{q_0}$              &$s_{10}$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_2=\set{q_1}$              &$s_{10}$  &$s_0$  \\
                    $s_3=\set{q_2}$              &$s_0$     &$s_7$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_4=\set{q_3}$              &$s_4$     &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_5=\set{q_0,q_1}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_0,q_2}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_7$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_7=\set{q_0,q_3}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_8=\set{q_1,q_2}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_7$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_9=\set{q_1,q_3}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{10}=\set{q_2,q_3}$          &$s_4$    &$s_7$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{11}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$      &$s_{10}$  &$s_7$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{12}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$      &$s_{10}$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{13}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_3}$      &$s_{10}$  &$s_7$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{14}=\set{q_1,q_1,q_3}$      &$s_{10}$  &$s_7$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{15}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_3}$  &$s_{10}$  &$s_7$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
For instance this repeats the computation above.
\begin{equation*}
  \Delta_D(s_1,\str{a})
    =\Delta_N(q_0,\str{a})\cup\Delta_N(q_3,\str{a})
    =\set{q_2} \cup \set{q_3}
    =\set{q_2,q_3}=s_{10}
\end{equation*}
For another example,
suppose that the machine is in $s_2=\set{q_1}$ and is reading \str{a}.
Following the $\varepsilon$ to $q_0$ and then reading \str{a} leads to
$q_2$.
Again starting in $q_1$, following the $\varepsilon$ to $q_0$
and following the other $\varepsilon$ to $q_3$,
and then reading the \str{a} leads to~$q_3$.
\begin{align*}
  \Delta_D(s_2,\str{a})
    &=\Delta_N(q_1,\str{a})\cup\Delta_N(q_0,\str{a})\cup\Delta_N(q_3,\str{a}) \\
    &=\set{}\cup\set{q_2} \cup \set{q_3}
    =s_{10}
    \end{align*}
    
A state is accepting if it contains $q_3$ but is also accepting
if it contains $q_0$ or $q_1$ since from those we can reach $q_3$ by
$\varepsilon$ transitions.
This machine's start state is $s_7=\set{q_0, q_3}$,
because from $q_0$ we can reach $q_3$ via an
$\varepsilon$~transition.
\end{example}

    $q_1$   &$\set{q_3}$  &$\set{q_3}$  \\

gives the next-state function for a nondeterministic Finite State machine.
