\begin{exercise}
A grammar is \definend{right linear} if every production rule has the
form \mbox{A\produces \trm{b}C}, where the right side has
a single terminal $\str{b}\in\Sigma$ followed by
a single nonteminal.
From this right linear grammar
\begin{grammar}
S \produces{} \trm{a} A

A \produces{} \trm{a} A \syntaxor \trm{b} B

B \produces{} \trm{b} B \syntaxor b
\end{grammar}
we can get this nondeterministic Finite State machine.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm68.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item Give three strings from the language of the grammar
and show that they are accepted by the machine.
\item Give three strings from the language of the machine,
different than the ones produced for the prior part,
and show that they are in the language of the grammar.
\item Describe the language of the grammar and the machine.
\item Give a right linear grammar for the language of this machine.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm69.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Decide whether each problem is solvable or
unsolvable  (by a Turing machine).
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang_{DFA}=\set{\sequence{\FSM,\sigma}\suchthat
         \text{the deterministic Finite State machine $\FSM$ accepts $\sigma$}}$
\item $\lang_{NFA}=\set{\sequence{\FSM,\sigma}\suchthat
         \text{the nondeterministic machine $\FSM$ accepts $\sigma$}}$
\item $\lang=\set{\FSM\suchthat
         \text{the Finite State machine $\FSM$ accepts at least one string}}$
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
  Rebut the contention, ``Epsilon transitions don't make any sense,
  they cause contradictions, because this machine will never get its first
  step done; it just endlessly following the epsilon transitions.''
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm59.pdf}
  \end{center}
  \begin{answer}
    We define that a string is accepted by a machine if there is a sequence
    of states that allow the machine to process the string, and that ends
    in an accepting state.
    That definition does not concern itself with wrong ways to go, it only
    requires that there be a right way.
    For instance, this machine accepts \str{aab} by starting in $q_0$,
    transitioning to $q_1$ on the first \str{a},
    taking the $\varepsilon$ transition back to $q_0$,
    then going to $q_1$ on the second~\str{a}, and finally
    going to $q_2$ with the \str{b}.
    The language accepted by the machine is defined to be the set of strings
    that the machine can accept, and so 
    the language of this machine is
    $\lang=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Given a nondeterministic Finite State machine without $\varepsilon$ transitions,
and with two accepting states, show how to add $\varepsilon$
transitions so that the extended machine will have only one accepting state,
without changing the accepted language. 
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% \Cpageref{par:NFSMAccepted} has a description of
% when an input is accepted by a
% nondeterministic Finite State machine~$\FSM$ without $\varepsilon$ transitions.
% Here is a definition that more formal, and constructive,
% meaning that you could follow it to
% write a program to compute whether the string is accepted.
% We define the \definend{extended transition function} 
% $\map{\extended{\Delta}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{\powerset(Q)}$
% giving the set of states that the machine will be in after starting in the
% given state and consuming the given string.
% (So \cref{anim:NondeterministicComputationTree} illustrates the
% computation of $\hat{\Delta}(q_0,\str{00001})$.)
% Where the input string is empty
% $\sigma=\emptystring$,
% for each state $q\in\FSM$ let $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=\set{q}$.
% Where the input string is not empty,
% peel off the final character, $\sigma=\sigma_0\concat x$ where $x\in\Sigma$,
% and for each $q\in Q$ define $\hat{\Delta}(\sigma)$ by:
% \begin{equation*}
%   \hat{\Delta}(q,\sigma)=
%     \hat{\Delta}(q_{i_0},x)\cup\hat{\Delta}(q_{i_1},x)\cup \,\cdots\,\hat{\Delta}(q_{i_k},x)
% \end{equation*}
% where $\Delta(q,\sigma_0)=\set{q_{i_0},q_{i_1},\, \ldots\,q_{i_k}}$.
% Then where $F_{\FSM}$ is the set of final states,
% the language of the machine is 
% $\lang(\FSM)=\set{\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
%   \hat{\Delta}(q_0,\tau)\cap F_{\FSM}\neq\emptyset}$.
% \begin{exparts}
% \item For the machine of \cref{ex:NDFMFromDiscussion},
%   find $\hat{\Delta}(q,\emptystring)$ for each $q\in Q$.
% \item Find $\hat{\Delta}(q,\str{0})$
%   and $\hat{\Delta}(q,\str{1})$ for each $q\in Q$.
% \item Find $\hat{\Delta}(q,x)$ for each $q\in Q$ and $x\in\B^2$.
% \item Continue until you find
%   $\hat{\Delta}(q,x)$ for each $q\in Q$ and $x\in\B^5$.
% \end{exparts}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:DefnEpsilonClosure}
  \Cref{ex:NFSMEpsilonClosure} illustrated following $\varepsilon$ transitions.
  We can give a rigorous definition.
  For each state $q\in Q$ we will define the
  \definend{$\varepsilon$ closure} $E(q)\subseteq Q$.
  The definition is inductive, so we get a sequence of sets
  $E_0(q)\subseteq E_1(q)\subseteq \cdots E_k(q)=E(q)$.
  The intiution is that $E_i(q)$ is the set of states reachable
  from~$q$ by traversing $i$ or fewer $\varepsilon$ edges.
  Formally, the definition's base step is that for each state, $E_0(q)=\set{q}$.
  The inductive step starts with $E_i(q)=\set{q_{i_0},\,\ldots q_{i_j}}$
  for some $i\in\N$ and produces $E_{i+1}(q)$
  \begin{equation*}
    E_{i+1}(q)=E_i(q)\cup \Delta(q_{I_0},\varepsilon)\cup\,\cdots\,
                         \Delta(q_{i_j},\varepsilon)
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item For the machine of \cref{ex:NFSMEpsilonClosure},
    for each $q\in Q$ produce $E_0(q)$,
    $E_1(q)$, $E_2(q)$, and~$E_3(q)$.
    List $E(q)$ for each $q\in Q$.
  \item Do the same for \cref{exer:MFSMWithEpsilonTrans}'s machine.
  \item This machine has loops of $\varepsilon$'s.
    \begin{center}
      \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm31.pdf}
    \end{center}
    Does the definition just keep going; what is $E(q)$ for each
    $q\in Q$?
  \item Prove that for every machine, $E(q)$ is defined for every $q\in Q$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}





% ==================================================
\section{Regular expressions}
\margingraphic{0.15\textwidth}{automata/pix/kleene.jpg}{\label{fig:kleene}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Cole_Kleene}{Stephen Cole Kleene 1909--1994}}
In 1951, S~Kleene\footnote{%
  \citetext{Pronounced KLAY-nee.}{His son Ken Kleene, wrote,
  ``As far as I am aware this pronunciation is incorrect in all known languages.
  I believe that this novel pronunciation was invented by my father.''
  \autocite{fodc:Kleene}}} 
was studying a
\citetext{mathematical model of neurons}{\autocite{wiki:ArtificialNeuron}}.
Neurons do not have any scratch memory.
They just respond to input
signals arriving from the other neurons to which they are connected.
Kleene abstracted some aspects of the definition of other researchers to get
a definition of Finite State machines.

He noted patterns to the languages that are accepted by such machines.
One of these patterns is the star operator that was later
named after him.
He gave the definition in the first subsection below,
and justified that with the theorem in the second subsection.

\subsection{Definition}
This machine
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa14.pdf}
\end{center}
accepts strings that
have some number of \str{a}'s, followed by one~\str{b},
followed by some number of \str{a}'s.
We now give a convenient way, called regular expressions, to
denote constructs such as ``any number of'' and ``followed by.''
% We will see strings, called Regular Expressions, that
% describe languages like this one,
% languages that are accepted by some Finite State machine.
%
We will introduce these with 
a few preliminary examples so that the definition will be clearer when it comes.
These use the alphabet $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\ldots \str{z}}$.

%<*ex:RegexOne>
\begin{example}
The string \re{h(a|e|i|o|u)t}
is a regular expression that 
describes the language where each word starts with~\str{h},
\citetext{has a vowel in the middle}{%
  Most speakers of American English cite the vowels as `a', `e', `i',
  `o', and~`u'.  
  See \autocite{Bigham}.},
and ends with~\str{t}.
The pipe `|' is a kind of `or'.
Hence, this regular expression
describes the language consisting of five words of three letters
each: $\lang=\set{\str{hat}, \str{het}, \str{hit},\str{hot}, \str{hut}}$.

There are two subtleties with this regular expression.
First, the parentheses are not part of the strings that are to be matched, they
provide grouping.
Second, besides the pipe operator and parentheses
the regular expression also uses concatenation since
the initial \str{h} is concatenated with \re{(a|e|i|o|u)},
which in turn is concatenated with \str{t}.
\end{example}
%</ex:RegexOne>

%<*ex:RegexTwo>
\begin{example}
The regular expression \re{ab*c} describes the language 
whose words begin with $\str{a}$, followed by any number of $\str{b}$'s
(including possibly zero-many $\str{b}$'s), and ending with  
a single~$\str{c}$.
So the~`$*$'  %`$\kleenestar{}$'
means `repeat the prior thing any number of times'.
This regular expression describes the language
$\lang=\set{\str{ac}, \str{abc}, \str{abbc},\, \ldots}$.
\end{example}
%</ex:RegexTwo>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:RegexABOrCStar}
There is an interaction between pipe and star.
The language described by 
the regular expression \re{a(b|c)*} consists of 
words starting with $\str{a}$, and ending with any number of
$\str{b}$'s or $\str{c}$'s.
It does not mean `any number of \str{b}'s or any number of \str{c}'s'.  
Rather, the two letters are mixed
$\lang=\set{\str{a}, \str{ab}, \str{ac}, 
             \str{abb}, \str{abc}, \str{acb}, \str{acc},\, \ldots}$.

In contrast, to describe the language 
whose words begin with \str{a} and end with any number of \str{b}'s
or any number of \str{c}'s but there is no mixing 
$\hat{\lang}=\set{\str{a},
  \str{ab}, \str{abb},\,\ldots\,,\str{ac},\str{acc},\,\ldots}$,
use the regular expression \re{a(b*|c*)}. 
\end{example}

\begin{example}
For the machine that opened this subsection, the language is described by
the regular expression \re{a*ba*}.
\end{example}

% So a regular expression is a short string that
% describes a language by describing 
% the pattern of the strings in that language.
% We are now ready for a formal definition.

% Their syntax is straightforward.
% Start with characters of an alphabet~$\Sigma$ (and the special characters
% $\emptystring$ and $\emptyset$ that we will describe below), and
% combine them using concatenation,
% pipe, and star.

\begin{definition} \label{def:RESyntax}
%<*def:RESyntax>
  Let $\Sigma$ be an alphabet not containing any of the metacharacters
  \re{)}, \re{(}, \re{|}, or \re{*}.
  A \definend{regular expression}
  over~$\Sigma$ is a string that can be derived from this grammar
\begin{grammar}
\ntrm{regex} \produces{} \ntrm{concat}
                    \othersyntax \ntrm{regex} `\trm{|}' \ntrm{concat}

\ntrm{concat} \produces{} \ntrm{simple}
                         \othersyntax \ntrm{concat} \ntrm{simple}

\ntrm{simple} \produces{} \ntrm{char}
                       \othersyntax  \ntrm{simple} \trm{*}
                       \othersyntax \trm{(} \ntrm{regex} \trm{)}
                       
\ntrm{char} \produces{} $\emptyset$ \syntaxor \emptystring   
                    \othersyntax $x_0$ \syntaxor $x_1$ \syntaxor \ldots{}
\end{grammar}
where the $x_i$ characters are members of the alphabet 
$\Sigma$.\footnotemark[1] 
%</def:RESyntax>
\end{definition}\footnotetext[1]{%
  As we often do in grammars,
  we use the pipe symbol~\re{|} as a metacharacter,
  to collapse rules with the same left side.
  But pipe also appears in regular expressions themselves.
  For that usage
  it is wrapped in single quotes.}

We don't want lots of parentheses so we must establish operator
precedence: star~\re{*} binds most tightly, then concatentation, then
the pipe alternation operator~\re{|}.
We use parentheses when needed, as in \cref{ex:RegexABOrCStar}.

As to their semantics, what they mean,
we will define that recursively.
%<*discussion:RESemantics>
The language described by the single-character
regular expression \re{$\emptyset$} is
the empty set, $\lang(\emptyset)=\emptyset$.
The language described by the regular expression consisting
of only the character~\re{$\emptystring$}
is the one-element language consisting of only the empty string, 
$\lang(\varepsilon)=\set{\emptystring}$.
If a regular expression consists of just one character from the alphabet
then the language it describes by contains only one string and that
string has only that single character:
where $\str{a}\in \Sigma$,
we have $\lang(\str{a})=\set{\str{a}}$.

We finish by defining the semantics of the operations.
We denote the language associated with the regular expression~$R$
as $\lang(R)$.
Then the pipe symbol means the union of the languages
$\lang(R|\hat{R})=\lang(R)\cup\lang(\hat{R})$.
Concatenation of the regular expressions
means concatneation of the languges
$\lang(R\concat\hat{R})=\lang(R)\concat\lang(\hat{R})$.
And, the star of the regular expression  
describes the Kleene star of the language,
$\lang(\kleenestar{R})=\kleenestar{\lang(R)}$.
%</discussion:RESemantics>

\begin{example}
Let $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
Consider the regular expression \re{aba*}.  
It is the concatenation of \re{a}, \re{b}, 
and \re{a*}.
The first describes the single-element language $\lang(\str{a})=\set{\str{a}}$.
Likewise the second describes $\lang(\str{b})=\set{\str{b}}$.
Together, the string \re{ab} describes the concatenation of the languages.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang(\str{a})\lang(\str{b})
  =\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma=\sigma_0\concat\sigma_1$ where $\sigma_0\in\lang(\str{a})$ and $\sigma_1\in\lang(\str{b})$}}
  =\set{\str{ab}}
\end{equation*}
The regular expression \re{a*} describes
$\lang(\re{a*})=\set{\str{a}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
Concatenating it to the language in the prior equation gives this.
\begin{align*}
  \lang(\re{aba*})
  &=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma=\sigma_0\concat\sigma_1$ where $\sigma_0\in\lang(\re{ab})$
      and $\sigma_1\in\lang(\re{a*})$}}             \\
  &=\set{\str{ab}\str{a}^n\suchthat n\in\N}
\end{align*}
\end{example}

We will illustrate with some constructs that appear often in building regular 
expressions.
These examples use $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$.

%<*ex:RegexThree>
\begin{example}
The language consisting of strings of \str{a}'s whose length is a multiple of
three
$\lang=\set{a^{3k}\suchthat k\in\N}=\set{\emptystring,\str{aaa},\str{aaaaaa},\,\ldots}$ is described by the
regular expression \re{(aaa)*}.
\end{example}
%</ex:RegexThree>

Note that the empty string is a member of that language.
A common gotcha is to forget that star is for any number of repetitions,
including zero-many.

%<*ex:RegexFour>
\begin{example}
The language of strings that have any number
of \str{a}'s and optionally end in one~\str{b},
$\lang=\set{\emptystring,\str{b},\str{a},\str{ab},\str{aa},\str{aab},\ldots}$,
is described by
the regular expression \re{a*($\emptystring$|b)}.
 
Similarly, to describe the language consisting of words with 
between three and five $\str{a}$'s,
$\lang=\set{\str{aaa}, \str{aaaa}, \str{aaaaa}}$,
we can use \re{aaa(\emptystring|a|aa)}.
\end{example}
%</ex:RegexFour>

\begin{example}
%<*ex:RegexFive>
To match any character we can list them all.
The language consisting of three-letter words ending in \str{bc} is
$\set{\str{abc}, \str{bbc}, \str{cbc}}$.
The regular expression \re{(a|b|c)bc} describes it.
%</ex:RegexFive>
(If the alphabet is large then listing all characters is
impractical; see the Extra section on Extended Regular Expressions.)
\end{example}

%<*ex:RegexSix>
\begin{example}
The language
$\set{\str{b},\str{bc},\str{bcc},
             \str{ab},\str{abc},\str{abcc},
             \str{aab},\,\ldots}$
consisting of words starting with any number of $\str{a}$'s
(including possibly zero-many \str{a}'s),
followed by a single~$\str{b}$, and then ending in fewer than three
$\str{c}$'s is described by 
\re{a*b(\emptystring|c|cc)}. 
\end{example}
%</ex:RegexSix>




% -----------------------------------------
\subsection{Kleene's Theorem}
Study of regular expressions is justified by the next result, that
they describe the languages of interest.

%<*th:KleenesTheorem>
\begin{theorem}[Kleene's Theorem] \label{th:KleenesTheorem}
  A language is accepted by a Finite State machine if and only if that
  language is described by a regular expression.
\end{theorem}
%</th:KleenesTheorem>

We will prove this in separate halves.
These halves use nondeterministic machines but since we can convert
those to deterministic machines, the result holds for those machines.

%<*lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine>
\begin{lemma} \label{lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine}
  If a language is described by a regular expression
  then there is a Finite State machine that accepts that language.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine>

The proof follows the steps suggested in \cref{ex:NFSMJoinParallel}, 
\cref{ex:NFSM:JoinSerial}, and \cref{ex:EpsilonTransMakeKleeneStar}.

\begin{proof}
  We will show that for any regular expression~$R$ there is a machine that
  accepts strings matching that expression.
  We use induction on the structure of regular expressions.

%<*pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachinei>
  Start with regular expressions consisting of a single character.
  If 
  $R=\re{$\emptyset$}$ then  $\lang(R)=\set{}$ and
  the machine on the left below accepts~$\lang(R)$.
  If $R=\re{$\emptystring$}$ then $\lang(R)=\set{\emptystring}$
  and the machine in the middle accepts this language.
  If the regular expression is a character from the alphabet,
  such as $R=\re{\str{a}}$, then a machine like the one on the right works.
%</pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachinei>
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm32.pdf}
    \hspace{5em}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm33.pdf}
    \hspace{5em}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm34.pdf}
  \end{center}

%<*pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachineii>
  To finish, we handle the three operations.
  % Assume that $R$ is constructed as in \cref{def:RESyntax} and
  % take the inductive hypothesis that its components satisfy the lemma.
  % There is a case for each operation.
  First consider alternation; suppose that 
  $R=\re{$R_0$|$R_1$}$, where $R_0$ and~$R_1$ are regular.
  By the inductive assumption there
  is a machine $\FSM_0$ whose language is described by
  $R_0$ and a machine~$\FSM_1$ whose language is described by $R_1$.
  Create the machine accepting the language described by $R$ by
  joining those two machines in parallel:~introduce
  a new state $s$ and use $\varepsilon$
  transitions to connect~$s$ to the start states of $\FSM_0$ and $\FSM_1$. 

  The next case is that
  $R=R_0\concat R_1$.
  The inductive hypothesis gives
  a machine for each, $\FSM_0$ and~$\FSM_1$.
  Join those two serially:~for
  each accepting state in $\FSM_0$, make
  an $\varepsilon$ transition to the start state of $\FSM_1$
  and then convert all those accepting states of $\FSM_0$ to be
  non-accepting states. 
  
  The final case is that
  $R=\re{($R_0$)*}$.
  % As in \cref{ex:EpsilonTransMakeKleeneStar},
  Take the machine~$\FSM_0$ accepting $R_0$ and for each accepting state
  that is not the start state, make an $\varepsilon$ transition to
  the start state.
  Make the start state an accepting state.
%</pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachineii>
\end{proof}

%<*lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex>
\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex}
  Any language accepted by a Finite State machine 
  is described by a regular expression. 
\end{lemma}
%</lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex>

Our strategy is to start with a Finite State machine and eliminate its states
one at a time, keeping the accepted language the same.
Below are before and after pictures of a part of a larger machine
where we eliminate the
state~$q$.
\begin{center}
\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm35.pdf}
\hspace{3em}
\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm36.pdf}
\end{center}
In the after picture the edge is labelled~\re{ab}, with more
than just one character.
For the proof we will generalize transition graphs to allow
edge labels that are regular expressions.
We will eliminate states until there are only two left, with one edge
between them, and that edge will be labelled with the desired expression.

Before the proof, an example.
Consider the machine on the left below.
Take \re{A}, \re{B}, \re{C}, and~\re{D} to be
regular expressions.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm64.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm65.pdf}}
\end{center}
The proof starts as above on the right
by introducing a new start state guaranteed to have
no incoming edges, $e$,
and a new final state guaranteed to be unique,~$f$.
Then the proof eliminates $q_1$ as below.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm66.pdf}}
\end{center}
Clearly the accepted language here is the same as that of the
starting machine.

\begin{proof}
  Call the machine~$\FSM$.
  If it has no accepting states then
  the regular expression is \re{$\emptyset$} and we are done.
  Otherwise, we will first transform the machine to a new one
  $\hat{\FSM}$, with the same language, on which
  we can execute the state-elimination strategy.

  First we arrange that
  $\hat{\FSM}$ has a single accepting state.
  Create a new state $f$ and
  for each of $\FSM$'s accepting states make an edge connecting it to~$f$
  (by the prior paragraph there is at least one such accepting state). 
  Label those edges with $\varepsilon$.
  Convert all the accepting states to non-accepting states
  and make~$f$ accepting.

  Next do a similar thing for the start state.
  Introduce a new start state~$e$.
  Give it an edge leading to $\FSM$'s~$q_0$
  labelled with $\varepsilon$.
  (Ensuring that $\hat{\FSM}$ has at least
  two states is a technical convenience to
  handle machines of all sizes uniformily.)

  Because the edge labels are regular expressions, we can arrange~$\hat{\FSM}$
  so that leading from any~$q_i$ to any~$q_j$
  is at most one edge; 
  if $\FSM$ has more than one edge then use the pipe~\re{|} to combine their
  labels.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm39.pdf}}
    \hspace{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm40.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Do the same with loops, that is, cases where $i=j$.
  This transformation, like the prior ones,
  clearly does not change the language of accepted strings.
  
  The last part of transforming to $\hat{\FSM}$ is to
  drop any useless states.
  If a state node~$q\neq f$ has no outgoing edges then
  drop it, along with the edges that come into it.
  The language of the machine will not change because this state
  cannot lead to a string being accepted\Dash it doesn't lead anywhere so
  it cannot lead to an accepting state, and $q$ cannot itself be an accepting
  state as only~$f$ is an accepting state.

  And, if a state node~$q$ is not reachable from the start~$e$ then we
  can drop that node along with its incoming and outgoing edges.
  (Construct the set of reachable states, and with it the set of
  unreachable states, in the natural way.
  Begin with the start state $S_0=\set{e}$.
  Find all states reachable from $e$ in a single transition
  by following every edge out of~$e$, $\varepsilon$ or not,  to get
  $S_1=\set{e,q_{1,0}, \ldots\,q_{1,k}}$.
  Then iterate:~starting with a set~$S_i$ of nodes that are
  reachable in $i$-many steps,
  for each $q\in S_i$ follow each outbound edge for a single step.
  The collection of the nodes reached in this way is~$S_{i+1}$.
  Stop the iteration
  when $S_i=S_{i+1}$, at which point it is the set of ever-reachable
  states.
  The unreachable ones are the others.)
  
  With that, we have $\hat{\FSM}$ and
  we are set up for the state elimination strategy.
  Below are the before and after pictures of the elimination of one state.
  (\citetext{This is the general version}{%
  This diagram is derived from \autocite{HopcroftUllman}.}
  of the example before the proof.)
  The before picture applies to any  $q\in\hat{\FSM}$
  with $q\neq e$ and $q\neq f$.
  It has at least one incoming and and at least one outgoing edge, by the
  setup work above.
  It might also have a loop.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm37.pdf}}
    \hspace{2em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm38.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  There is a tricky point here:~possibly some of the state nodes shown on the
  left of each of the two
  pictures equal some shown on the right.
  For example, in the before picture possibly $q_{i_0}$ equals~$q_{o_0}$.
  If so then the shown edge $R_{i_0,o_0}$ is a loop.

  Eliminate $q$ and the associated edges by making the replacements
  shown in the after picture.
  Observe that the set of strings taking the machine from any incoming
  node~$q_i$ to any outgoing node~$q_o$ is unchanged.
  So the language accepted by the machine is unchanged.
  
  Perform this elimination for every node that is not $e$ or~$f$.
  There are only finitely many states so this procedure must 
  eventually stop.
  Then, all that remains are $e$, $f$, and an edge.
  The edge label is the desired regular expression.
\end{proof}

% \begin{example}
% Consider~$\FSM$ on the left.
% We can take the edge labels \re{A}, \re{B}, \re{C}, and~\re{D} to be
% regular expressions, or perhaps characters from the tape alphabet.
% Introduce $e$ and~$f$ to get $\hat{\FSM}$ on the right.
% \begin{center}
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm64.pdf}}
%   \hspace{2em}
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm65.pdf}}
% \end{center}
% We will eliminate~$q_1$.

% So $\hat{\FSM}$ relates to the key diagram's left side as: $q$ is~$q_1$,
% there is one node originating an edge into $q_1$  so
% $q_{i_0}$ is~$q_0$, and there is one node that receives an
% edge leaving $q_1$ so $q_{o_0}$ is~$q_2$.
% The regular expressions are
% $R_{i_0}=\re{A}$, $R_\ell=\re{B}$, $R_{o_0}=\re{C}$,
% and there is no edge for the regular expressions~$R_{i_0,o_0}$.
% Carrying these to the diagram's right side gives that the edge
% between $q_{i_0}$ and~$q_{o_0}$ labelled
% \re{$R_{i_0,o_o}$|$R_{i_0}$$R_\ell$*$R_{o_0}$},
% becomes \re{D|AB*C}.
% \begin{center}
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm66.pdf}}
% \end{center}
% \end{example}

\begin{example}
  Consider~$\FSM$ on the left.
  Introduce $e$ and~$f$ to get $\hat{\FSM}$ on the right.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm48.pdf}}
    \hspace{2em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm49.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Start by eliminating $q_2$.
  In the terms of the proof's key step, $q_1=q_{i_0}$
  and $q_0=q_{o_0}$.
  The regular expressions are $R_{i_0}=\re{a}$,
  $R_{o_0}=\re{b}$,
  $R_{i_0,o_0}=\re{b}$,
  and $R_{\ell}=\re{b}$.
  That gives this machine.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm50.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Next eliminate $q_1$.
  There is one incoming node
  $q_0=q_{i_0}$
  and two outgoing nodes $q_0=q_{o_0}$ and~$f=q_{o_1}$.
  (Note that $q_0$ is both an incoming and outgoing node; this is the
  tricky point mentioned in the proof.)
  The regular expressions are
  $R_{i_0}=\re{a}$,
  $R_{o_0}=\re{b|(ab*b)}$,
  and $R_{o_1}=\re{$\varepsilon$}$.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm51.pdf}}    
  \end{center}
  Almost done.
  We only have to eliminate $q_0$.
  The sole incoming node is $e=q_{i_0}$
  and the sole outgoing node is $f=q_{o_0}$,
  and so $R_{i_0}=\re{$\varepsilon$}$,
  $R_{o_0}=\re{$\varepsilon$|a$\varepsilon$}$,
  and
  $R_{\ell}=\re{$\varepsilon|a(b|ab*b)$}$.
  (That regular expression simplifies.
  For instance, \re{a$\varepsilon$}=\re{a}.)
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm52.pdf}}    
  \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
  For each regular expression produce, if possible, five bitstrings that match
  and five that do not.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item \re{01*}
  \item \re{(01)*}
  \item \re{1(0|1)1}
  \item \re{(0|1)($\emptystring$|1)0*}
  \item \re{$\emptyset$}
  \end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  For these regular expressions, look at each element of
  $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$ that is of length at most $3$ and
  decide if it is a match.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item \re{a*b}
  \item \re{a*}
  \item \re{$\emptyset$}
  \item \re{$\emptystring$}
  \item \re{b(a|b)a}
  \item \re{(a|b)($\emptystring$|a)a}
  \end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  For these regular expressions, decide if each element of
  $\kleenestar{\B}$ of length at most~$4$ is a match.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item \re{0*1}
  \item \re{1*0}
  \item \re{$\emptyset$}
  \item \re{$\emptystring$}
  \item \re{0(0|1)*}
  \item \re{(100)($\emptystring$|1)0*}
  \end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
Give a regular expression to describe each language over $\B$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The set of strings of odd parity, where the number of \str{1}'s is
  odd.
\item The set of strings where no two adjacent characters are equal.
\item The set of strings representing in binary multiples of eight.
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Give a regular expression for the set of bitstrings whose number of \str{0}'s
  is even.
  Give a regular expression for the set of bitstrings whose number of
  \str{1}'s is greater than 2.
  Give a regular expression for the set of bitstrings whose number of \str{0}'s
  is even and whose number of \str{1}'s is greater than 2.
  \begin{answer}
  For the first, \re{(1*01*01*)*}.
  For the second, \re{0*11*0*11*0*11*(0|1)*}.
  For the last, use this.
  \begin{equation*}
    (1*01*01*)*(1|010)1*(1*01*01*)*(1|010)1*(1*01*01*)*(1|010)1*((1*01*01*)*(1|010)1*)*
  \end{equation*}
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a regular expression to describe each language over
the alphabet $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$.

\item The set of strings ending with \str{aba}.
\item The set of strings containing the substring \str{aba}.
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}

  by a \str{b}.
\item Each string has at least two \str{b}'s that are not followed by an
  \str{a}.
\item Each string has no more than one pair of consecutive \str{a}'s
  and no more than one pair of consecutive \str{b}'s.
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Produce a regular expression for the language of bitstrings
  such that the number of \str{1}'s is a multiple of four.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Give a regular expression to accept each language.

  \item
    $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
      \text{$\sigma$ ends with the same symbol it began with, and $\sigma\neq\emptystring$}}$

\begin{exercise}
Give a regular expression for each language over $\kleenestar{\B}$.

\item The strings representing a binary number that is a multiple of eight.
\item The bitstrings where the first character differs from the final
one.
\item The bitstrings where no two adjacent characters are equal.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{1(0|1)*000}
\item \re{1(01)*0|0(10)*1}

\end{exercise}
\begin{exercise}

\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Apply the state elimination method of
\cref{lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex}'s proof to eliminate $q_1$.
Note that each of the states $q_0$ and $q_2$ are of the kind
described in the proof's comment on the tricky point.

First add the states $e$ and~$f$.

There is also an edge from $q_0$ to~$q_2$ labelled \re{AB*C},

\section{Regular languages}

\subsection{Definition}
Finite State machines are interesting, at least, because they give us insight
into what a machine can do without scratch memory.
We can get an idea of how powerful such a machine is by determining
the set of jobs that they can and cannot do.

%<*def:RegularLangs>
\begin{definition}
  A \definend{regular language} is one that is 
  accepted by some Finite State machine.
\end{definition}
%</def:RegularLangs>

There are infinitely many regular languages.
For one thing, every finite language $\lang_0=\set{}$,
$\lang_1=\set{\str{0}}$, $\lang_2=\set{\str{00}}$, \ldots{}
is regular; just list all the cases as in \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}.

If the language is finite but large then constructing the machine
by listing cases may not be practical.
For example,
there are finitely many people in the world and each has finitely many
active phone numbers so strictly speaking
the set of all currently-active phone numbers
is a regular language but constructing a Finite State machine for it
is 
\urldef\goldbachurl\url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldbach%27s_conjecture}%
silly.\footnote{A finite 
  regular language doesn't have to be large for it to be
  difficult, in some sense.
  For example, consider
  \citetext{\definend{Goldbach's conjecture}}{See \goldbachurl.}, that
  every even number greater
  than~$2$ is the sum of
  two primes, as in $4=2+2$, $6=3+3$, $8=3+5$, \ldots\@
  Computer testing shows the pattern holds up to very large
  numbers but no one knows if it is true for all evens.
  Now consider the set of
  strings $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\ldots\,\str{9}}}$
  representing the smallest even number
  that is not the sum of two primes.
  This set is finite since it has either
  one member or none.
  But while this set is tiny, we don't know what it contains.}

\subsection{Closure properties}
In the proof of Kleene's Theorem we saw that if two languages
are regular then their union is also regular.
We have also seen, in \cref{ex:NFSM:JoinSerial},
that if two languages are regular then their concatenation
is regular.
We say that 
\citetext{a structure is \definend{closed} under an operation if
performing that operation on its members always yields another member}{%
  Familiar examples are that adding two integers always gives an integer 
  so the integers are closed under the operation of addition, and 
  that squaring an integer always results in an integer
  so that the integers are closed under squaring.}.

%<*lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar>
\begin{lemma}
  The collection of regular languages is closed under
  union, concatenation, and Kleene star.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar>

\begin{proof}
  In proving \cref{lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine},
  the first half of Kleene's Theorem,
  we did the regular expression operations of pipe,
  concatenation, and Kleene star.
  Those correspond to these set operations:
  for instance, if $R_0$ is a regular expression describing the language
  $\lang_0$, and $R_1$ describes $\lang_1$, then the regular expression
  $\re{$R_0$|$R_1$}$ describes $\lang_0\cup\lang_1$.
\end{proof}


%<*th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>
\begin{theorem}  \label{th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff}
  The collection of regular languages is closed under
  set complement, intersection, and set difference. 
\end{theorem}
%</th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>

%<*pf:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>
\begin{proof}
  The  equation
  $S\cap \hat{S}=\setcomp{(\setcomp{S}\cup\setcomp{\smash{\hat{S}}\hspace{1.5pt}})}$
  describes set intersection in terms of union and complement.
  The prior lemma shows that regular languages are closed under union,
  so if we show they are closed under complement then this equation
  gives that they are also closed under intersection.
  Similarly, 
  $S-\hat{S}=S\cup\setcomp{\smash{\hat{S}}\hspace{1.5pt}}$ gives that
  showing closure under complement will show closure under difference.

  So to show that the complement of a regular language
  is also regular, fix a regular language~$\lang$.
  It is accepted by some Finite State machine~$\FSM$.
  \Cref{sec:EquivDFSMAndNFSM} shows that deterministic and nondeterministic
  Finite State machines recognize the same set of languages so without
  loss of generality take~$\FSM$ to be deterministic.
  
  Define a new machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ with the same states and
  transition function as~$\FSM$ but whose accepting states are the
  complement,
  $F_{\hat{\FSM}}=Q_{\FSM}-F_{\FSM}$.
  Because $\FSM$ is deterministic, each input string~$\tau$ is associated with a
  unique last state,~$q_{\tau}$, the state the machine is in after it
  consumes $\tau$'s last character.
  Moving from $\FSM$ to $\hat{\FSM}$ by taking the complement of the
  accepting states will complement the set of accepted strings, the language
  of the machine, because $q_{\tau}\in F_{\FSM}$ if and only if
  $q_{\tau}\notin F_{\hat{\FSM}}$.
  Thus the language of $\hat{\FSM}$ is the
  complement of the language of $\FSM$ and
  since this language is accepted by a Finite State machine, it too
  is regular.
\end{proof}
%</pf:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:RegLangEvenZerosMoreThanFourOnes}
  This language is regular.
  \begin{equation*}
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has an even number of \str{0}'s and more than two \str{1}'s}}
  \end{equation*}
  One way to show that is to produce a Finite State machine.
  Another is to spot that~$\lang$ is the intersection of 
  $\lang_0=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has an even number of \str{0}'s}}$
  and
  $\lang_1=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has more than two \str{1}'s}}$.
  Producing machines for those two is easy.
\end{example}

% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/100215  
We now know that regular expressions, regular grammars,
deterministic finite state machines and nondeterministic finite state
machines all describe the same set of languages, namely, the regular
languages.
The fact that we can describe these languages in so many completely different
ways suggests that there's something natural and important about these
languages.
This is in analogy with the fact that the equivalence of Turing machines,
the lambda calculus and all kinds of other things suggests that the
computable languages are natural and important.
They're not just an artifact of whatever random decisions the original
definer made.


\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
True or false?
Of course you must justify each answer.
\begin{exparts}
\item Every regular language is finite.
\item Over $\kleenestar{\B}$, the empty language is not regular.
\item Over $\kleenestar{\B}$, the language of all strings, $\kleenestar{\B}$,
  is not regular.
\item Every regular language is countable.
\item Every Finite State machine accepts at least one string.
\item For every Finite State machine there is one that has fewer
  states but accepts the same language.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item False.
The language $\set{\str{a}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$ is regular, and is infinite.
\item False, the empty language is accepted by any Finite State machine
that has no accepting states.
\item False, the set of all strings is accepted by any Finite State machine
whose states are all accepting states.
\item False.
Over $\kleenestar{\B}$ the language of all strings $\lang=\kleenestar{\B}$
is regular, and is uncountable.
\item False, a Finite State machine with no accepting states does not
accept any strings.
\item False, a minimal machine cannot be further reduced.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One of these is true and one is false.
Which is which?
\begin{exparts*}
\item Any finite language is regular.
\item Any regular language is finite.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
The first is true and the second is false.
\begin{exparts}
\item
If the language consists of the strings $\sigma_0$, \ldots $\sigma_{n-1}$
then a regular expression is
\re{$\sigma_0$|$\cdots$|$\sigma_{n-1}$}.
\item
The language over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ described by the
regular expression \re{a*} is
$\set{\emptystring,\str{a},\str{aa},\ldots}$, and is infinite.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false?
Justify your answer.
\begin{exparts}
\item If $\lang_0$ is a regular languages and $\lang_1\subseteq \lang_0$ then
  $\lang_1$ is also a regular languages.
\item  If $\lang_0$ is not regular and $\lang_0\subseteq \lang_1$ then
  $\lang_1$ is also not regular.
\item If $\lang_0\cap\lang_1$ is regular then each of the two is
  regular.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
Each is false.
\begin{exparts}
\item For $\lang_0$ use the set of all bitstrings, $\kleenestar{\B}$.
  For $\lang_1$ use any non-regular subset of it, such as
  $\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
\item For $lLang_0$ use any non-regular set of bitstrings, such as
  $\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  For $\lang_1$ use $\kleenestar{\B}$.
\item For $\lang_0$ take any nonregular set of bitstrings, such as 
  $\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  For $\lang_1$ take its complement inside of $\kleenestar{\B}$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
    Suppose that the language $\lang$ over $\B$ is regular.
    Show that the language
    $\hat{\lang}=\set{1\concat\sigma\suchthat \sigma\in\lang}$,
    also over~$\B$, is also regular.
\end{exercise}

  
  \begin{exercise}  % Rich p183
    Prove that the collection of regular languages over $\Sigma$
    is closed under each of the operations.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item $\operatorname{pref}(\lang)=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{there is a $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ such that $\sigma\concat\tau\in\lang$}}$ 
      \item $\operatorname{suff}(\lang)=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{there is a $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ such that $\tau\sigma\in\lang$}}$ 
    \end{exparts}    
  \end{exercise}

    \begin{exercise}
    The proof of \cref{th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff} works with
    deterministic Finite State machines.
    Find a nondeterministic Finite State machine $\FSM$ such that
    producing another machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ by
    taking the complement of the accepting states,
    $F_{\hat{\FSM}}=\setcomp{(F_{\FSM})}$,
    will not result in the langugage of the second machine
    being the complement of the language of the first.
    \begin{answer}
      For this machine the language of accepted strings contains~\str{1}.
      \begin{center}
        \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm57.pdf}
      \end{center}
      If we complement the set of accepting states then the language
      of the resulting machine also contains~\str{1}.
    \end{answer}
  \end{exercise}

  \begin{exercise}
  Show that the set of regular languages is countable.
  \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the language accepted by a Finite State machine with~$n$ states is 
infinite if and only if that machine accepts at least one string of length $k$,
where $n\leq k <2n$.  
\end{exercise}
  
  \begin{exercise}
    Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma\neq \emptyset$.
    Is the set of regular languages over~$\Sigma$ infinite?
    If so, is it countable?
    Is the set of languages over~$\Sigma$ that are not regular infinite?
    If so, is it countable?
  \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix two alphabets $\Sigma_0,\Sigma_1$.
A function $\map{h}{\Sigma_0}{\kleenestar{\Sigma_1}}$ induces a
\definend{homomorphism} on~$\kleenestar{\Sigma_0}$ via the operation
$h(\sigma\concat\tau)=h(\sigma)\concat h(\tau)$
(and $h(\emptystring)=\emptystring$).
\begin{exparts}
\item Take $\Sigma_0=\B$ and $\sigma_1=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$.
  Fix a homomorphism $\hat{h}(\str{0})=\str{a}$ and $\hat{h}(\str{1})=\str{ba}$.
  Find $\hat{h}(\str{01})$, 
  $\hat{h}(\str{10})$, 
  and $\hat{h}(\str{101})$.
\item Define $h(\lang)=\set{h(\sigma)\suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma_0}}$.
  Let $\hat{\lang}=\set{\sigma\concat\str{1}\suchthat\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$;
  describe it with a regular expression.
  Using the homomorphism~$\hat{h}$ from the prior item, describe 
  $\hat{h}(\hat{\lang})$
  with a regular expression.
\item Prove that the collection of regular languages is closed under
  homomorphism, that if $\lang$ is regular then so is $h(\lang)$.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item $\hat{h}(\str{01})=\str{aba}$, 
  $\hat{h}(\str{10})=\str{baa}$,
  $\hat{h}(\str{101})=\str{baaba}$
\item A regular expression to describe 
  $\hat{\lang}=\set{\sigma\concat\str{1}\suchthat\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$
  is \re{(\str{0}|\str{1})*\str{1}}
  A regular expression to describe
  $\hat{h}(\hat{\lang})$ is \re{(a|ba)*ba}.
\item Let $\lang$ be described by the regular expression~$R$.
  Apply the function~$h$ to each non-meta symbol in~$R$.
  The result is a regular expression that describes $h(\lang)$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
  \begin{exercise}
    We will show that the class of regular languages is closed under
    reversal.
    Recall that the reversal of the language is
    defined to be the set of reversals of the strings in the language
    $\reversal{\lang}=\set{\reversal{\sigma}\suchthat \sigma\in\lang}$.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item Show that for any two strings the reversal of the concatenation
      is the concatenation, in the opposite order, of the reversals
      $\reversal{(\sigma_0\concat\sigma_1)}
         =\reversal{\sigma_1}\concat\reversal{\sigma_0}$.
    \textit{Hint:} you can use induction on the length of $\sigma_1$.
  \item One way to prove the result
    is to define a reversal operation on regular expressions.
    Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma$.
    Let $\mathcal{R}$ be the set of regular expressions over that
    alphabet.
    Then we can define each of these:
    (i)~$\reversal{\emptyset}=\emptyset$
    (ii)~$\reversal{\emptystring}=\emptystring$
    (iii)~$\reversal(x)=x$ for any~$x\in\Sigma$
    (iv)~$\reversal{(R_0\concat R_1)}=\reversal{R_1}\concat\reversal{R_0}$
    (v)~$\reversal{\re{$R_0$|$R_1$}}=\re{$\reversal{R_0}$|$\reversal{R_1}$}$
    (vi)~$\reversal{\re{$R$*}}=\re{$\reversal{R}$*}$.
    (Note the relationship between (iv) and the prior exercise item.)
    Now show that this operation turns a regular expression for a language
    into a regular expression for the language's reversal.
  \item
    Another way to prove this is to reverse the machine.
    Take the language to be accepted by some machine~$\FSM$.
    Construct a new nondeterministic Finite State machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ by:
    (i)~giving $\hat{\FSM}$ all of $\FSM$'s states, so that
    $Q_{\hat{\FSM}}=Q_{\FSM}$.
    (ii)~adding to $\hat{\FSM}$ a new state~$s$, make $\varepsilon$
    transitions from it to all of $\FSM$'s accepting states,
    and make $s$ the start state of~$\hat{\FSM}$
    (iii)~reverse all of the transitions in the $\FSM$'s graph, 
    and
    (iv)~turn $\FSM$'s accepting states to non-accepting states, and
    turn $\FSM$'s start state to an accepting state.
    Show that $\hat{\FSM}$ accepts $\reversal{\lang}$.
  \end{exparts}
  \end{exercise}

  \begin{exercise}
  Consider the set of powers of two $\set{2^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Show that there is a Finite State machine that accepts the
  collection of strings representing powers of~$2$ in binary.
  \item Show that there is no Finite State machine that accepts the
  collection of strings representing these numbers in decimal.
  \end{exparts}
  \begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item These strings have an inital~\str{1} and any subsequent characters are
  \str{0}'s.
  \item \url{https://cstheory.stackexchange.com/q/2083}
  \end{exparts}
  \end{answer}
  \end{exercise}

\end{exercises}





\section{Non-regular languages}
Although Finite State machines are finite,
they can handle arbitrarily long input strings.
For instance, \Cref{ex:PowerSwitch}'s power switch machine has
only two states
but even if we toggle it hundreds of times, it still knows whether
in aggregate it is off or on.

Experience with Kleene's star operator gives the intuition that 
a Finite State machine handles long strings, as the power switch does,
by looping.
We will see a result expressing that and we will
use it for proofs that languages are not regular.

\begin{example}
%<*ex:FSMLoopingi>
Imagine a finite state machine that accepts
the string $\sigma_1=\str{abaacb}$.
As the machine processes the string, it passes from state to state;
imagine they are the ones here (there may be many other
states but this only shows the ones that the machine traverses).
%</ex:FSMLoopingi>
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm58.pdf}
\end{center}
%<*ex:FSMLoopingii>
Part of the processing includes
a loop from $q_{i_1}$ to~$q_{i_2}$, to~$q_{i_3}$, and back to~$q_{i_1}$.
We can get another accepted string by making the machine go
around the loop twice, 
$\sigma_2=\str{a}(\str{baa})^2\str{cb}$.
Likewise, this machine accepts $\sigma_3=\str{a}(\str{baa})^3\str{cb}$,
and looping more times pumps out more
members of the machine's language.

In addition, bypassing the loop entirely, going around zero times times by
feeding the machine $\sigma_0=\str{acb}$, also ends in the accepting
state~$q_{i_5}$.
%</ex:FSMLoopingii>
\end{example}

%<*th:PumpingLemma>
\begin{theorem}[Pumping Lemma]
Let~$\lang$ be a regular language.
Then there is a constant~$p\in\N$, the
\definend{pumping length} for the language,
such that every string~$\sigma\in \lang$ with $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$
decomposes into three components
$\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ satisfying:
(i)~the first two components are short, $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$,
(ii)~$\beta$ is not empty,
and (iii)~all of these strings $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
$\alpha\beta^3\gamma$, \ldots{} are also members of
the language.
\end{theorem}
%</th:PumpingLemma>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:PumpingLemmai>
  This is the Pigeonhole Principle:~suppose that $\lang$ is accepted by the
  Finite State machine~$\FSM$,
  and that $\FSM$ has $p$~states.
  If we give the machine an input string with more than~$p$ characters
  then in processing that string
  the machine will visit some state more than once.
  Said more completely, recall that the
  extended transition function $\extended{\Delta}$ inputs a string
  and outputs the state to which that string brings the machine.
  Consider an input
  $\sigma=\sequence{s_0,s_1,\ldots s_k}$
  with more characters than the machine has states, $k\geq p$.
  Then the sequence of states through which the machine passes
  in processing~$\sigma$
  \begin{equation*}
    \extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0}),\;  
    \extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,s_1}),\;
    \extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,s_1,s_2}),\;
    \ldots\quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,s_1,\ldots s_k})
  \end{equation*}
  must have a repeat.
%</pf:PumpingLemmai>

%<*pf:PumpingLemmaii>
  So suppose that the two inputs $\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i}$ and
  $\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_j}$ with $j>i$
  both take the machine to the same ending state, 
  $q=\extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i})
     =\extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_j})$.
  Then let $\alpha=\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots, s_i}$,
  let $\beta=\sequence{s_{i+1},\,\ldots s_j}$, and
  let $\gamma=\sequence{s_{j+1},\,\ldots s_k}$
  (possibly one or both of $\alpha$ and $\gamma$ is empty).
  These clearly satisfy conditions~(i) and~(ii).
  For~(iii),
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha\concat\gamma=\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i,s_{j+1},\,\ldots s_k}
  \end{equation*}
  brings the machine from the start state~$q_0$ to the state~$q$,
  and then to the same final state as did $\sigma$.
  That is,
  $\extended{\Delta}(\alpha\gamma)=\extended{\Delta}(\alpha\beta\gamma)$.
  The other strings in~(iii) are similar.
  For instance, in
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha\concat\beta^2\concat\gamma
      =\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i,s_{i+1},\,\ldots, s_{j-1},s_{i+1},\,\ldots s_{j+1},\,\ldots s_k}
  \end{equation*}
  $\alpha$ brings the machine from~$q_0$ to the state~$q$,
  and the first $\beta$ brings it around to~$q$ again,
  then the second $\beta$ makes the machine loop to $q$ yet again,
  and finally $\gamma$ brings it to the same final state as did $\sigma$.
%</pf:PumpingLemmaii>
\end{proof}

Typically we use the Pumping Lemma to show that a language is
not regular, by assuming that if it is regular and then using the lemma
to get an impossibility.


\begin{example}  \label{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang}
  We will show that this language of matched parentheses is not regular.
  \begin{equation*}
    \lang
    =\set{\varepsilon, \str{()}, \str{(())}, \str{((()))}, 
       \str{(}^4\mspace{2mu}\str{)}^4,\, \ldots\,}       
    =\set{\str{(}^n\str{)}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{)},\str{(}}}\suchthat
        n\in\N}                                      
  \end{equation*}
  For contradiction, assume that it is regular.
  Then the Pumping Lemma says that it has a pumping length~$p$.

  Consider the string $\sigma=\str{(}^p\str{)}^p\in\lang$.
  Its length is greater than or equal to~$p$ so
  the Pumping Lemma says that it
  decomposes into three substrings $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$
  satisfying the conditions.
  Condition~(i) is that
  the length of the prefix~$\alpha\concat\beta$ is less than or equal to~$p$.
  Thus both of the substrings $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ are composed
  exclusively of open parentheses,~$\str{(}$'s.
  Condition~(ii) is that $\beta$ is not the empty string, so it
  contains at least one~$\str{(}$.

  Condition~(iii) is that all of the strings
  $\alpha\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^3\gamma$,
  \ldots{}
  are members of~$\lang$.
  We will find a contradiction by considering $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$.
  Compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, this string has an extra~$\beta$.
  That adds at least one open parenthesis without adding any balancing closed
  parentheses.
  In short, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ has more \str{(}'s than \str{)}'s. 
  So it is not a member of the language~$\lang$ of balanced parentheses, which 
  contradicts the Pumping Lemma.
  Therefore the assumption that $\lang$ is regular is incorrect.
\end{example}

Being able to show that a language is not regular gives a nice
balance to the ability we already have to show that a language is regular,
either by producing a Finite State machine that accepts it or
by producing a regular expression that describes it.
But more than symmetry motivates our interest.
Since a Turing machine can solve the problem of accepting
strings of balanced parentheses but the example says that a
Finite State machine cannot,
we now know that to solve the problem we need scratch memory.
So the results here speak to problems, and the resources needed to solve them.

\begin{example}
Recall that a palindrome
is a string that reads the
  same backwards as forwards, such as
  \str{bab}, \str{abbaabba},
  or~$\str{a}^5\str{b}\str{a}^5$.
  We will prove that the language
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
    \reversal{\sigma}=\sigma}$
  of all palindromes over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ is
  not regular.

  For contradiction assume that this language is regular. 
  The Pumping Lemma says that $\lang$ has a pumping length.
  Call it~$p$.
  
  Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{a}^p\in\lang$.
  That string has more than~$p$ characters so it decomposes as
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the three conditions.
  Condition~(i) is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$
  and so both substrings $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are composed entirely of
  \str{a}'s.
  Condition~(ii) is that $\beta$ is not the empty string and so
  $\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{a}.
  
  Condition~(iii) is that the strings in the list
  $
    \alpha\gamma,   
    \alpha\beta^2\gamma,
    \alpha\beta^3\gamma,
    \ldots{}
  $ are also members of the language.
  We will get the contradiction by showing that the first one, $\alpha\gamma$,
  is not a palindrome.
  Because $\alpha$ and $\beta$ consist entirely of \str{a}'s, $\gamma$ has
  the~\str{b}.
  By the definition of $\sigma$, after that~\str{b} must be $\str{a}^p$.
  For $\alpha\gamma$ to be a palindrome, the~\str{b} must be preceeded by
  $p$-many \str{a}'s.
  But $\alpha\gamma$ has fewer than $p$-many \str{a}'s before
  \str{b} because, compared to $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, the $\beta$ is gone.
  So $\alpha\gamma$ is not a palindrome.
  This is a contradiction because the Pumping Lemma says that the strings
  in the list are all members of~$\lang$.
  So the assumption that $\lang$ is regular is wrong.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
Don't make the mistake of thinking that in that example
the prefix $\alpha\beta$ is necessarily all of~$\str{a}^p$. 
The Pumping Lemma only gives that $\alpha\beta$ has length
less than or equal to~$p$.
The suffix $\gamma$ could well start with some $\str{a}$'s,
in addition to containing~$\str{b}\str{a}^p$.
\end{remark}

We introduced Finite State machines as stripped down Turing machines,
as ones without a tape so they are simpler.
A person might guess that the simpler machine type recognizes
simpler languages.
But that intuition is misleading.
Most people would informally judge that the language from
\cref{ex:RegLangEvenZerosMoreThanFourOnes} is more complex
than the following one, although the first is regular while the second is not.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSMCannotRecognizePlusOne}
  The language
  $\lang=\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^n\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
    m=n+1}$
  is very simple.
  Its members, such as \str{0},
  \str{001}, and~\str{00011}, just have one more \str{0} than~\str{1}.
  We will prove that it is not regular, that no Finite State machine
  accepts this language.

  Assume otherwise.
  Let the language's pumping length be~$p$ and
  consider the string $\sigma=\str{0}^{p+1}\str{1}^p\in\lang$.
  The Pumping Lemma gives the decomposition
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the three conditions.
  Condition~(i) says that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, so $\alpha$ and~$\beta$
  are made up only of \str{0}'s.
  Condition~(ii) says that $\beta$ has at least one character, necessarily
  a~\str{0}.
  Condition~(iii) says that each of these is in the language.
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha\gamma,
    \quad  
    \alpha\beta\gamma,
    \quad  
    \alpha\beta^2\gamma,
    \quad  
    \alpha\beta^3\gamma,
    \quad\ldots
  \end{equation*}
  Consider $\alpha\gamma$.
  By conditions~(i) and~(ii), all of the \str{1}'s are in~$\gamma$.
  By condition~(ii), this string has fewer \str{0}'s then does
  $\sigma$.
  According to the definition of~$\lang$ that's impossible.
\end{example}

The examples so far all have the same flavor.
We can also use the Pumping Lemma for differently-flavored cases.

\begin{example}
The language
$  \lang  =\set{\str{a}^n\suchthat \text{$n$ is a perfect square} } 
         =\set{\emptystring,\, \str{a},\, \str{a}^4,\, \str{a}^9,\,\str{a}^{16},
                \, \ldots\,}$
is not regular.
% \begin{equation*}
%   \lang  =\set{\str{a}^n\suchthat \text{$n$ is a perfect square} } 
%          =\set{\emptystring,\, \str{a},\, \str{aaaa},\, \str{a}^9,\,\str{a}^{16},
%                 \, \ldots\,}
% \end{equation*}
For, suppose otherwise.  
Fix a pumping length~$p$ and consider $\sigma=\str{a}^{(p^2)}$.

By the Pumping Lemma, $\sigma$ decomposes into
$\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the
three conditions.
Condition~(i) 
is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, which implies that $\lh{\beta}\leq p$.
Condition~(ii) is that $0<\lh{\beta}$.
Condition~(iii) say that all of the strings  $\alpha\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}
are members of the language.
Consider $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$.
Because $p^2=\lh{\alpha\beta\gamma}$ we have
\begin{equation*}
  \lh{\alpha\beta^2\gamma}
  =p^2+\lh{\beta}  
  \leq p^2+p       
  <p^2+2p+1=(p+1)^2
\end{equation*}
and so the length of $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ is not a perfect square, which is
a contradiction.
\end{example}

We can solve some problems by using the Pumping Lemma in conjunction with
the closure properties of regular languages.

\begin{example}
  We can prove that 
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has as many \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s}}$
  is not regular.
  The language
  $\hat{\lang}=\set{\str{a}^m\str{b}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat m,n\in\N}$ is clearly
  regular, and the intersection of two regular languages is regular.
  But $\lang\cap\hat{\lang}$ is the set
  $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$
  and \cref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang} shows it isn't regular,
  by substituting the letters for the parentheses.
\end{example}


% We can informally interpret the prior example
% to be that Finite State machines cannnot square.
% Along the same lines, 
% \cref{ex:FSMCannotRecognizePlusOne} says Finite State machines
% cannot recognize the successor operation and
% \cref{exer:NoFSMAcceptsPrimes} says that they
% cannot recognize primality.
% % And,
% % \cref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang}
% % is notable because compilers must recognize whether the input
% % has matched parentheses.
% These results tell us in some sense what the CPU part of a Turing machine
% cannot do without scratch memory.  

\begin{exercises}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:BalancedParenFirstStringNoGood}
\Cref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang} uses $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$
to show that the language of
balanced parentheses is not regular.
Instead get the contradiction by showing that the first string on the list,
$\alpha\gamma$, is not a member of the language.
\begin{answer}
The omission of $\beta$ means that it has at least one fewer
open parenthesis~\str{(} than closed parenthesis~\str{)}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
    Show that each language is not regular.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item $\lang_0=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\str{c}^n\suchthat n,m\in\N}$
    \item $\lang_1=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\suchthat n+2=m}$
    \item $\lang_2=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\suchthat n<m}$
    \end{exparts}    
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that the language over $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$
consisting of strings having more \str{a}'s than \str{b}'s is not
regular.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One of these is regular and one is not.
Which is which?
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat n,m\in\N}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat 3<m}$
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Which of these languages is regular and which is not?
Of course, you must prove each assertion.
Assume $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{\sigma\concat\sigma\suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}}$.
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat n,m\in\N}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat n+3=m}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat m-n>12}$
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is this language regular or not:
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B} suchthat \sigma=\alpha\beta\reversal{\alpha}}$.
Either way, prove it.
\begin{answer} % David Richerby https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/97885/67754
The language is regular.
It is a trick question:~take $\alpha=\emptystring$ to get that the set equals
$\kleenestar{\B}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{1}}}
            \suchthat \text{$\lh{\sigma}=n!$ for some $n\in\N$}}$
is not regular.
\end{exercise}  
  
\begin{exercise}
One of these is regular, one is not.
Which is which?
(Of course, you must prove your assertions.)
\begin{equation*}
\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}\str{0}^n\suchthat m,n\in\N}
\qquad
\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}\str{0}^n\suchthat n\in\N}
\end{equation*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % Rich, p 182
Decide if each is a regular language of bitstrings.
\begin{exparts*}
\item the number of \str{0}'s plus the number of \str{1}'s equals five
\item the number of \str{0}'s minus the number of \str{1}'s equals five
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

  
\begin{exercise}
Show that
$\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^n\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat m\neq n}$
is not regular.
\textit{Hint:} use the closure properties of regular languages.
\end{exercise}

    
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:NoFSMAcceptsPrimes}
Show that 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
\text{$\sigma=\str{1}^n$ where $n$ is prime}}$
is not a regular language.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Nondeterministic Finite State machines can always be made to have a single
accepting state.
For deterministic machines that is not so.
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that any Deterministic Finite State machine that accepts the
  finite language
  $\lang_1=\set{\emptystring,\str{a}}$ must have at least two accepting states.
\item Show that any Deterministic Finite State machine that accepts 
  $\lang_2=\set{\emptystring,\str{a},\str{aa}}$ must have at least three
  accepting states.
\item Show that for any natural number $n$ there is a regular language
  that is not recognized by any Deterministic Finite State machine
  with at most~$n$ final states.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
% This is not right.
% \begin{exparts}
% \item It must have at least one accepting state because it accepts some strings.
% If it had only one accepting state
% then accepting \str{a} would mean that the same path through
% the machine taken twice would result in it accepting \str{aa}.
% \item This answer is similar to the last one.
% If it accepts \str{aa} then the same path through the machine taken twice would
% lead it to accept \str{aaaa}.
% \item Consider $\lang_n=\set{\emptystring,\str{a},\ldots,\str{a}^n}$.
% The path through the machine that leads to accepting $\str{a}^n$,
% if taken twice, would lead to accepting $\str{a}^{2n}$.
% \end{exparts}
% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/30726
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}







% ...................................................
\section{Minimization}
Contrast these two Finite State machines.
For each, the language of accepted strings is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has at least one \str{0} and at least one \str{1}}}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa16.pdf}
  \hspace{3em}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa17.pdf}
\end{center}
Our experience from building machines is that the states should have a meaning.
For instance, on the left $q_2$ means something like,
``have seen the needed at least one \str{1} but still
waiting for a~\str{0}.''
In a properly designed machine, input puts the machine in state~$q_2$
if indeed that input has so far had at least one~\str{1} but no~\str{0}. 

On the right the machine is not properly designed in this sense because
the meaning of $q_4$ is the same as that of~$q_2$.
That is, the two states have the same future.
The machine on the right has redundant states.

\begin{definition}  \label{def:distinguishable}
In a Finite State machine over~$\Sigma$,
where $n\in\N$ we say that two states $q,\hat{q}$ are
\definend{$n$-distinguishable}
if there is a string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ with
$\lh{\sigma}\leq n$ such that starting the machine
in state~$q$ and giving it input~$\sigma$ ends in an accepting state
while starting it in~$\hat{q}$ and giving it~$\sigma$ does not,
or vice versa.
Otherwise the states are \definend{$n$-indistinguishable, $q\sim_{n}\hat{q}$}.

Two states $q,\hat{q}$ are \definend{distinguishable}
if there is an~$n$ for which they are $n$-distinguishable.
Otherwise they are \definend{indistinguishable, $q\sim\hat{q}$}.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
  Starting the machine on the left above in state~$q_0$
  and feeding it~$\sigma=\str{0}$ ends
  in the non-accepting state~$q_1$,
  while starting it in~$q_2$ and processing the same input ends
  in the accepting state~$q_3$.
  So $q_0$ and~$q_2$ are $n=1$-distinguishable, and therefore are
  distinguishable.
  
  Also, $q_2$ and $q_3$ are $0$-distinguishable, via~$\sigma=\emptystring$.
  Any state that is not accepting is $0$-distinguishable from any
  state that is accepting.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
  For the machine on the right,
  the states $q_2$ and~$q_4$ are indistinguishable.
  The set of input strings that bring the
  machine from~$q_2$ to an accepting state is
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has the form \re{0*|(1*0*)}}}$.
  (Below we will see an algorithm but here we just work by inspection.)
  For state~$q_4$ the set of strings taking
  it to an accepting state satisfies the same regular expression.
  
  The states that the machine is brought to
  need not be equal, it is a matter of whether they are accepting or not.
  For instance, if $\sigma=\str{0}$
  then starting at $q_2$ takes the
  machine to $q_3$ while starting at $q_4$ takes the machine to $q_5$.
  This is not a distinguishing string because, while the states aren't
  equal, both are accepting.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:StateEquivIsEquivRel}
  The $\sim$ relation and the $\sim_{n}$ relations
  are equivalences.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \cref{exer:NEquivIsEquivRel}.  
\end{proof}

\citetext{We will give an algorithm}{%
  This is
  \definend{Moore's algorithm}.  It is easy to understand and suitable for
  small calculations but if you are writing code then be
  aware that another algorithm also exists, \definend{Hopcroft's algorithm}.
  See \autocite{Knuutila}.}
that starts with a Finite State machine and
finds from it the smallest machine that accepts the same language
by removing the redundancies,
by collapsing together indistinguishable states.
This algorithm first finds all states that are
distinguishable by the length~zero string.
It next finds all states that are distinguishable by length zero or one
strings, etc.
At the end, the machine's staes have been broken into classes where
inside each class all of the states are indistinguishable.

The machine below is the same as the one we saw earlier,
where we remarked that state~$q_4$ is redundant on~$q_2$.
By the same token, $q_5$ is redundant on~$q_3$.
\begin{center} \label{page:MachineWithRedundantStates}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa17.pdf}
\end{center}
Consider first states that are indistinguishable by the length zero
string~$\emptystring$.
They fall into two classes,
the non-accepting states $Q-F=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}$
accepting states $F=\set{q_3,q_5}$.
We use this notation for the equivalence classes
of the $\sim_{0}$~relation.
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{0,0}=Q-F=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}
  \quad
  \eclass_{0,1}=F=\set{q_3,q_5}
\end{equation*}

Next we find the classes of states that are $1$-indinstinguishable, that
are indistinguishable by either length zero or length one strings.
For each state we find where putting the machine in that state
and feeding it the length one strings \str{0} and~\str{1} will take
the machine.
We only care whether the resulting state is final. 
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[]
    &\str{0}  &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$  &$Q-F$    &$Q-F$    \\
    $q_1$  &$Q-F$    &$F$    \\
    $q_2$  &$F$      &$Q-F$    \\
    $q_3$  &$F$      &$F$    \\
    $q_4$  &$F$      &$Q-F$     \\
    $q_5$  &$F$      &$F$    \\      
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
Some states are distinguishable, and some are not.
For instance, $q_0$ and $q_1$ are $1$-distinguishable
by the input string~\str{1}.
And 
both $q_0$ and~$q_1$ are $1$-distinguishable from $q_2$, by~\str{0}.
But $q_2$ and~$q_4$ are $1$-indistinguishable.
These are the $\sim_{1}$ equivalence classes.
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
The redundant states continue to be
in the same class.

To get the $\sim_{2}$ classes
we may first think to pass each length two string to each state.
Proceeding in this way, then passing the collection of length
three strings, etc., would mean that the job grows exponentially
since there are $2^k$ strings of length~$k$.
However:~if two states $q,\hat{q}$
are $2$-distinguishable but not $1$-distinguishable
then where $\sigma$ is a distinguishing string,
these two states must be distinguished by $\sigma$'s final character
because if an earlier character would do then they would be
$1$-distinguishable.
In general, let two states $q,\hat{q}$ be
$n+1$-distinguishable but not $n$-distinguishable and
suppose that a distinguishing string is 
$
 \sigma=\sequence{s_0,s_1,\,\ldots s_{n-1},s_n}=\sigma_0\concat s_n 
$.
If $\sigma_0$ brings the machine from $q$ to a state in some
class then
$\sigma_0$ must bring the machine from $\hat{q}$ to the same class
or else the states $q$ and $\hat{q}$ would be $n$-distinguishable.
It is the final character~$s_n$ that distinguishes the two states.
Therefore, we need only test single characters
to see if they split the equivalence class  $\eclass_{n,i}$'s.

Continuing with this example, we calculate whether the classes from
equation~($*$) split any further.
These are the relevant subsets of the table.
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[]
    &\str{0}  &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_2$      &$F$    &$Q-F$    \\
    $q_4$      &$F$    &$Q-F$    
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \qquad  
  \begin{transitionfunction}[]
    &\str{0}  &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_3$      &$F$    &$F$    \\
    $q_5$      &$F$    &$F$    
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
In neither case does the class split,
so we stop. 

\begin{example}
  We will find a machine that accepts the same language as this one but
  that has a minimimum number of states.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min03.pdf}
  \end{center}
  To do bookkeeping we will use a sequence of tables like the one below.
  They have an entry for 
  every two-element set of state indices $\set{i,j}$
  with $i,j\in\set{0,\,\ldots 5}$ and  $i\neq j$.
  
  Start by marking the $i,j$~entries for
  state pairs that are $0$-distinguishable, that is, 
  where one of $q_i,q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{center} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &    &    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{center}
   The blanks entries
   mark state pairs that are $0$-indistinguishable.
   So~$\sim_{0}$, which as a binary relation is a set of pairs,
   is below.
   (The table doesn't bother with the $q_i,q_i$ boxes, because
   a state is indistinguishable from itself.
   In the relation below these are written $\set{q_0,q_0}$. 
   Of course in a set repeats collapse so we could rewrite, e.g.,
   $\set{q_0,q_0}$ as $\set{q_0}$,
   but as written they are correct and also fit with
   \cref{lem:CorrectnessOfMooresAlgorithm}.)
   \begin{multline*}
     \sim_{0}=\bigl\{\set{q_0,q_0},\,\set{q_0,q_3},\,\set{q_0,q_4},\,
       \set{q_3,q_3},\,\set{q_3,q_4},\,\set{q_4,q_4}, \bigr.       \\
     \bigl. \set{q_1,q_1},\,\set{q_1,q_2},\,\set{q_1,q_5},\,
     \set{q_2,q_2},\,\set{q_2,q_5},\,\set{q_5,q_5} \bigr\}
   \end{multline*}
   Form the $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes by 
   finding entries~$i,j$ that are blank, and then $q_i$ is $0$-equivalent
   to~$q_j$.
   For instance, the equivalence class containing $q_0$ also contains
   $q_3$ and~$q_4$ because the boxes $0,2$ and~$0,3$ are blank.
   Similarly, the equivalence class containing $q_2$ also contains $q_1$
   and~$q_5$.
   Here are the two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{0,3,4}
     \qquad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{1,2,5}        
   \end{equation*}

  Now iterate.
  Look at the table's blanks, the $0$-indistinguishable pairs of states,
  to see if they can be $1$-distinguished.
  On the left below is each $0$-indistinguishable pair along with the
  pair to which the machine takes it with the listed input character.
  For instance, the input \str{a} takes the machine's state $q_0$ 
  to~$q_1$, and takes $q_3$ to~$q_5$.
  If the resulting pair is $0$-distinguishable then mark it.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $\set{0,3}$  &$\set{1,5}$  &$\set{2,5}$  \\
      $\set{0,4}$  &$\set{1,5}$  &$\set{2,5}$  \\
      $\set{1,2}$  &$\set{3,4}$  &$\set{4,3}$  \\
      $\set{1,5}$  &$\set{3,5}$ \ck  &$\set{4,5}$ \ck  \\
      $\set{2,5}$  &$\set{4,5}$ \ck  &$\set{3,5}$ \ck  \\
      $\set{3,4}$  &$\set{5,5}$   &$\set{5,5}$  \\                              
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    % \begin{varwidth}{0.19\linewidth}
    %   \begin{rulealigned*}
    %     \eclass_{1,0} &=\set{0,3,4} \\
    %     \eclass_{1,1} &=\set{1,2}   \\ 
    %     \eclass_{1,2} &=\set{5}   
    %   \end{rulealigned*}
    % \end{varwidth}
    \equivclasses{%
        \eclass_{1,0} &=\set{0,3,4} \\
        \eclass_{1,1} &=\set{1,2}   \\ 
        \eclass_{1,2} &=\set{5}   
    }
  \end{center}
  So this round finds that $q_1$ is $1$-distinguishable from~$q_5$
  and that $q_2$ is $1$-distinguishable from~$q_5$.
  The triangular table shows the additional marks, and the
  $1$-equivalence classes also reflect that.

  Rinse, repeat.
  The next iteration subdivides the $\sim_{1}$-equivalence classes,
  the $\eclass_{1,i}$'s, to compute the $\sim_{2}$-equivalence classes.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $\set{0,3}$  &$\set{1,5}$ \ck  &$\set{2,5}$ \ck \\
      $\set{0,4}$  &$\set{1,5}$ \ck &$\set{2,5}$ \ck \\
      $\set{1,2}$  &$\set{3,4}$  &$\set{4,3}$  \\
      $\set{3,4}$  &$\set{5,5}$   &$\set{5,5}$  \\                              
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    % \begin{varwidth}{0.2\linewidth}
    %   \dropeqnvertspace
    %   \begin{align*}
    %     \eclass_{2,0} &=\set{0} \\
    %     \eclass_{2,1} &=\set{3,4} \\
    %     \eclass_{2,2} &=\set{1,2}   \\ 
    %     \eclass_{2,3} &=\set{5}   
    %   \end{align*}
    % \end{varwidth}
    \equivclasses{\eclass_{2,0} &=\set{0} \\
        \eclass_{2,1} &=\set{3,4} \\
        \eclass_{2,2} &=\set{1,2}   \\
