% \[\]]))|"(?:[^\"\r\\]|\\.|(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t]))*"(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*)(?:\.(?:(?:
% \r\n)?[ \t])*(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\031]+(?:(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])+|\Z|(?=[\[
% "()<>@,;:\\".\[\]]))|"(?:[^\"\r\\]|\\.|(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t]))*"(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])
% *))*@(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\031]+(?:(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])
% +|\Z|(?=[\["()<>@,;:\\".\[\]]))|\[([^\[\]\r\\]|\\.)*\](?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*)(?:\
% .(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\031]+(?:(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])+|\Z
% |(?=[\["()<>@,;:\\".\[\]]))|\[([^\[\]\r\\]|\\.)*\](?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*))*\>(?:(
% ?:\r\n)?[ \t])*))*)?;\s*)  
% \protect\end{lstlisting}}
We show here just five lines out of its~$81$ but that's enough to
make the point about its complexity.
\begin{lstlisting}
(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*(?:(?:(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\031]+(?:(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t]
)+|\Z|(?=[\["()<>@,;:\\".\[\]]))|"(?:[^\"\r\\]|\\.|(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t]))*"(?:(?:
\r\n)?[ \t])*)(?:\.(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\031]+(?:(?:(
?:\r\n)?[ \t])+|\Z|(?=[\["()<>@,;:\\".\[\]]))|"(?:[^\"\r\\]|\\.|(?:(?:\r\n)?[ 
\t]))*"(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*))*@(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\0
\end{lstlisting} % }{%

\noindent And, even if you do have an address that fits
the standard, you don't know if there is an email server listening at
that address.

At this point regular expressions may be starting to seem
a little less like a fast and neat problem-solver
and a little more like a potential development and maintenance problem.
The full story is that sometimes a regular expression is just what you need
for a quick job,
and sometimes they are good for more complex tasks also.
But some of the time the cost of complexity
outweighs the gain in expressiveness.
This power/complexity tradeoff is often referred to online by citing this
quote from
% \urldef\zawinski\url{https://groups.google.com/forum/?hl=en#!msg/alt.religion.emacs/DR057Srw5-c/Co-2L2BKn7UJ}
\citetext{J~Zawinski}{%
  The post is from \texttt{alt.religion.emacs} on 1997-Aug-12.
  For some reason it keeps disappearing from the online archive.}.

\smallskip
\begin{minipage}{0.5\textwidth}
\begin{quotation}\small
\noindent The notion that regexps are the solution to all
problems is \ldots{} braindead.
\ldots\@
Some people, when confronted with a problem, think "I know,
I'll use regular expressions."  
\citetext{Now they have two problems.}{%
  A classic example is trying to use regular expressions to parse
  significant parts of an HTML document.
  See \autocite{bobnice}.}
\end{quotation}
\end{minipage}\hspace*{1em}%
\begin{minipage}{0.45\textwidth}
% https://xkcd.com/1171/
  \begin{tabular}[t]{c}
    \includegraphics[scale=0.25]{automata/pix/xkcdperlproblems.png}  \\
    \href{https://xkcd.com/1171/}{Courtesy \texttt{xkcd.com}}
  \end{tabular}
\end{minipage}

% As with the postal codes, one pattern that you see 
% in practice if for programmers to make the engineering choice to use
% regular expressions to give something of a rough cut, something
% good-enough.
% But they have plenty of other uses also, such as validating
% new usernames, or input to a database.

% regex for Roman numerals: https://phvu.wordpress.com/2009/05/19/an-cfg-for-roman-numerals/
% '^M{0,4}(CM|CD|D?C{0,3})(XC|XL|L?X{0,3})(IX|IV|V?I{0,3})$'
\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
Which of the strings matches the regex
\re{ab+c}?
\begin{exparts*}
\item abc
\item ac
\item abbb
\item bbc
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Which of the strings matches the regex
\re{[a-z]+[\textbackslash .\textbackslash? !]}?
\begin{exparts*}
\item  battle!
\item Hot
\item green
\item swamping.\@
\item jump up.\@
\item undulate?
\item is.?
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give an extended regular expression to
accept a string for each description.
\begin{exparts}
\item Containing the substring \str{abe}.
\item Containing only upper and lower case ASCII letters and digits.
\item Containing a string of between one and three digits.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{.*abe.*}
Note that in practice you often can just write \re{abe}; see your
language's regular expression documentation.
\item \re{[a-zA-Z0-9]*}
\item \re{[\textasciicircum]}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Every lot of land in New York City is denoted by a string of digits called BBL,
for Borough (one digit), Block (five digits), and Lot (four digits).
Give a regex.
\begin{answer}
\re{\textbackslash d\{10\}}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\Cref{ex:NAPhoneNumber} gives a regex for North American phone numbers.
\begin{exparts}
\item They are sometimes written with parentheses around the area code.
  Extend the regex to cover this case.
\item Sometimes phone numbers do not include the area code.
  Extend to cover this also.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{(\textbackslash d\{3\}|\textbackslash (\textbackslash d\{3\}\textbackslash )) \textbackslash d\{3\}-\textbackslash d\{4\}}
\item We must factor out the space as well as the area code:
  \re{(\textbackslash d\{3\} |\textbackslash (\textbackslash d\{3\}\textbackslash ) )?\textbackslash d\{3\}-\textbackslash d\{4\}}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Most operating systems come with file that has a list of words, which can
be used for spell-checking, etc.
For instance, on Linux it may be at \str{/usr/share/dict/words} but
in any event you can find it by running
\str{locate words | grep dict}.
Use that file to find how many words fit the criteria.
\begin{exparts*}
\item contains the letter \str{a}
\item starts with~\str{A}
\item contains \str{a} or~\str{A}
\item contains \str{X}
\item contains \str{x} or~\str{X}
\item contains the string \str{st}
\item contains the string \str{ing}
\item contains an~\str{a}, and later a~\str{b}
\item contains none of the usual vowels \str{a}, \str{e}, \str{i}, \str{o}
  or~\str{u}
\item contains all the usual vowels
\item contains all the usual vowels, in ascending order
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a regex to accept time in a 24~hour format.
It should match times of the form `hh:mm:ss.sss' or
`hh:mm:ss' or `hh:mm' or `hh'.
\begin{answer}
\re{\textbackslash d\{2\}:\textbackslash d\{2\}:\textbackslash d\{2\}(.\textbackslash d +)|\textbackslash d\{2\}(:\textbackslash d\{2\})?}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Give an extended regular expression
% for ISO 8601 hours, minutes, seconds (with optional time zone).
% You can find an exact description on the web.
% \begin{answer}
% \re{\textasciicircum(2[0-3]|[01][0-9]):?([0-5][0-9]):?([0-5][0-9])(Z|[+-](?:2[0-3]|[01][0-9])(?::?(?:[0-5][0-9]))?)\$}
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a regex describing a floating point number.
\begin{answer}
One is \re{[-\textbackslash +]?(\textbackslash d)+\textbackslash .?|\textbackslash d)*\textbackslash .\textbackslash d+)}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a suitable extended regular expression.
\begin{exparts}
\item All Visa card numbers start with a \str{4}.
New cards have 16 digits. Old cards have 13
\item MasterCard numbers either start with \str{51} through \str{55},
or with the numbers \str{2221} through \str{2720}.
All have 16 digits.
\item American Express card numbers start with \str{34}
  or~\str{37} and have 15 digits. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\item \re{4(\textbackslash d{15}|\textbackslash d{12})}
\item \re{(5[1-5]\textbackslash d{2}|222[1-9]|22[3-9]\textbvackslash d|2[3-6]\textbackslash d{2}|27[01]\textbackslash d|2720)\textbackslash d{12}}
\item \re{3[47][0-9]{13}}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Postal codes in the United Kingdom have six possible formats.
They are: (i)~\str{A11 1AA},
(ii)~\str{A1 1AA},
(iii)~\str{A1A 1AA},
(iv)~\str{AA11 1AA},
(v)~\str{AA1 1AA},
and (vi)~\str{AA1A 1AA}, where
\str{A} stands for a capital ASCII letter and \str{1} stands for a digit.
\begin{exparts}
\item Give a regex.
\item Shorten it.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
We can do the six as alternatives.
\begin{center}
  \re{[A-Z]\d{2} \d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]\d \d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]\d[A-Z] \d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]{2}\d{2} \d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]{2}\d \d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]{2}\d[A-Z] \d[A-Z]{2}}
\end{center}
\item We can factor out the ending of \re{\d[A-Z]{2}}.
\begin{center}
  \re{[A-Z]\d{2}|[A-Z]\d|[A-Z]\d[A-Z]|[A-Z]{2}\d{2}|[A-Z]{2}\d|[A-Z]{2}\d[A-Z]) \d[A-Z]{2}}
\end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
You are stuck on a crossword puzzle.
You know that the first letter (of eight) is an \str{g},
the third is an \str{n}
and the seventh is an \str{i}.
You have access to a file that contains all English words, each on its own
line.
Give a suitable regex.
\begin{answer}
A suitable regex is \re{^g.n.{3}i.\textdollar} and a match is
`gynaecia', meaning the aggregate of carpels or pistils in a flower.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In the Downsides discussion of \cref{ex:AmerTime}, we change the ending to
\re{(\emptystring|\textbackslash s am|\textbackslash s pm)}.
Why not \re{\textbackslash s(\emptystring|am|pm)}, which
factors out the whitespace?
\begin{answer}
Factoring out the whitespace would mean that times without \str{am} or \str{pm}
must have a trailing whitespace.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The Roman numerals taught in grade school use the letters I, V, X, L, C, D,
and~M to represent 1, 5, 10, 50, 100, 500, and 1000.
They are written in descending order of magnitude, from M to~I, and are
written greedily so that we don't write six I's but rather VI.
Thus, the date written on the book held by the Statue of Liberty is 
MDCCLXXVI, for 1776.  
Further, we replace IIII with IV, and replace VIIII with IX.
Give a regular expression for valid Roman numerals less than 5000.
\begin{answer}
\re{\textasciicircum M{0,4}(CM|CD|D?C{0,3})(XC|XL|L?X{0,3})(IX|IV|V?I{0,3})\$}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:LangSquares}
\Cref{ex:LangSquares} says that the language of squares
over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}
         \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\tau\concat\tau$ for some $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$}}
\end{equation*}
is not regular.
Verify that.
\begin{answer}
Assume it is a regular language and let its pumping length be~$p$.
Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{a}^p\str{b}$
It is an element of~$\lang$ whose length is greater than~$p$ so
By the Pumping Lemma it decomposes as $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$
subject to the three conditions.
By the first condition the length of $\alpha\concat\beta$ is less than~$p$,
and so these two substrings consist soley of~\str{a}'s.
The second condition is that $\beta$ is not the empty string.
The third condition says that the strings
$\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} are all members of~$\lang$.
Note that the first is not a square because in omitting~$\beta$ it omits at
least one~\str{a} from the prefix but not from the suffix.
That's a contradiction.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider
$\lang=\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}\str{0}^n\suchthat n>0}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Show that it is not regular.
\item Find a regex.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item We take the alphabet to be $\Sigma=\B$.
Assume that it is regular and let its pumping length be~$p$.
Consider $\sigma=\str{0}^p1\str{0}^p$, which is a member of the language.
By the Pumping Lemma it therefore decomposes into $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$
subject to the three conditions.
By the first and second conditions $\alpha\beta$ consists solely of
str{0}'s, and is therefore part of the prefix before the~\str{1},
and $\beta$ is nonempty.
By the third condition the string $\alpha\gamma$ is also a member of~$\lang$.
But this is a contradition because the omission of $\beta$ means
that $\alpha\gamma$ has fewer \str{0}'s before the~\str{1} than after.
\item \re{(a*)b\textbackslash 1}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In \citetext{\definend{regex golf}}{See \url{https://alf.nu/RegexGolf}.}
you are given two lists and must produce a
regex that matches all the words in the first list but none of the
words in the second.
The `golf' aspect is that the person who finds the shortest regex, 
the one with the fewest characters, wins.
Try these: accept the words in the first list and not the words in the second.
\begin{exparts}
\item Accept: Arthur, Ester, le~Seur, Silverter

Do not accept: Bruble, Jones, Pappas, Trent, Zikle

\item Accept: alight, bright, kite, mite, tickle

Do not accept: buffing, curt, penny, tart

\item Accept: afoot, catfoot, dogfoot, fanfoot, foody, foolery, foolish,
fooster, footage, foothot, footle, footpad, footway, hotfoot, jawfoot,
mafoo, nonfood, padfoot, prefool, sfoot, unfool

Do not accept: Atlas, Aymoro, Iberic, Mahran, Ormazd, Silipan, altared,
chandoo, crenel, crooked, fardo, folksy, forest, hebamic, idgah, manlike,
marly, palazzi, sixfold, tarrock, unfold
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{.*r}
\item \re{.*i.*t.*}
\item \re{.*foo.*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In a \definend{regex crossword} each row and column has a regular expression.
You have to find strings for those rows and columns that meet the constraints.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
\begin{tabular}{r|c|c|}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\rotatebox{90}{\re{[\textasciicircum{}SPEAK]+}}}
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\rotatebox{90}{\re{EP|IP|EF}}}  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  \re{HE|LL|O+}  &  &     \\ \cline{2-3}   
  \re{[PLEASE]+} &  &     \\ \cline{2-3}
\end{tabular}
\item
\begin{tabular}{r|c|c|}

                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\rotatebox{90}{\re{(A|B|C)\textbackslash 1}}}
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\rotatebox{90}{\re{(AB|OE|SK)}}}  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  \re{.*M?O.*}  &  &     \\ \cline{2-3}   
  \re{(AN|FE|BE)} &  &     \\ \cline{2-3}
\end{tabular}

% \extra{Neural nets}
% \margingraphic{0.2\textwidth}{automata/mccullochpitts.png}{\label{fig:mcculloghpitts}\href{http://nautil.us/issue/21/information/the-man-who-tried-to-redeem-the-world-with-logic}{Warren McCulloch 1898--1969, Walter Pitts 1923--1969}}
% \begin{exercise}
% % Exercise http://djm.cc/fsquad/firing.html
% Myhill posed the firing squad synchronization problem in 1957.
% You are given a row of soldiers, each of which can be in a finite number
% of states.
% A clock provides a sequence of time steps, $0$, $1$, $2$, \ldots\,.
% At each positive time step, the state of each soldier is set to a function,
% the \textit{transition function},
% of its previous state and the state of each adjacent soldier.
% This function is identical for all soldiers not at the ends of the row;
% the end soldiers, however, may have different transition functions.
% (This is a \textit{cellular automaton}.)

% Three of the soldiers' states are called the
% \textit{quiescent},
% \textit{excited},
% and \textit{firing} states.
% At time $0$, all soldiers are in their quiescent states,
% except for one soldier at an end, who is in his excited state.
% The problem is to arrange the state sets of the soldiers and
% the transition functions such that for this starting position,
% no matter how many soldiers you start with, all soldiers will,
% at some future time, enter their firing states simultaneously. 

% \begin{answer}  
% % Ans
% One way to solve the problem is to have the excited state shoot out two projectiles, one of which moves at speed 1 and one of which moves at speed 1/3. If the speed 1 projectile is made to bounce off the row's end, these projectiles will meet in the middle of the row. The row can then be bisected, and this bisection step can be repeated to quadrisect, 8-sect, etc., the row. When all these segments vanish (which will happen simultaneously), the soldiers should enter their firing state.

% Here is such a solution, with 13 states, and here is an illustration of the solution running for 20, 40, 80, and 160 soldiers. (In these pictures, and all succeeding pictures, the quiescent state is black, the excited state is yellow, and the firing state is white.) Bisecting a row of length N by this method will take around 3N/2 time steps, so the whole solution will take about 3N/2+3N/4+...=3N time steps.

% How little time can a solution take? It takes 2N-2 time steps just for a signal to pass from one end of the row to the other and back, so any solution must take at least this much time. In fact there are solutions that take just 2N-2 time steps. Here is a minimal-time solution by Abraham Waksman (Information and Control 9 (1966), 66-78), and here are runs of it for 48, 96, 192, 384, and 768 soldiers.

% Waksman's solution uses 16 states. Here is a minimal-time solution with 11 states (David Karr, 1999) and runs of it for 20, 40, 80, and 160 soldiers; and here is a minimal-time solution by Robert Balzer with 8 states (Information and Control 10 (1967), 22-42) and runs of it for 20, 40, 80, and 160 soldiers. Finally, here is a minimal-time solution with just six states by Jacques Mazoyer (Theoretical Computer Science 50 (1987), 183-238), and runs of it for 40, 200, 500, and 1000 soldiers.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

% \extra{XML} %  SEE https://www.w3.org/TR/xml/#sec-notation
% \begin{center} % https://xkcd.com/1144/
%   \includegraphics[scale=0.50]{automata/xkcdtags.png}
% \end{center}


\extra{The Myhill-Nerode Theorem}
We defined regular languages in terms of Finite State machines.
Here we will give a characterization that does not depend on
that.

This Finite state machine accepts strings that end in \str{ab}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa18.pdf}  
\end{center}
Consider other strings over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$,
not just the accepted ones,
and see where they bring the machine.
\begin{center}  \small
  \begin{tabular}{r|*{12}{c}}
    \tabulartext{Input string $\sigma$}
    &$\emptystring$ &\str{a} &\str{b}
      &\str{aa} &\str{ab} &\str{ba} &\str{bb}
      &\str{aaa} &\str{aab} &\str{aba} &\str{abb}        \\
    \cline{2-12}
    \tabulartext{Ending state $\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$}
    &$q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$
      &$q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$
      &$q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$                 \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
The collection of all strings $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$,
pictured below,
breaks into three sets,
those that bring the machine to~$q_0$, those that bring the machine
to~$q_1$, and those that bring the machine to~$q_2$.
\begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{l}
    $\eclass_{\FSM,0}=\set{\varepsilon, \str{b},\str{bb},\, \str{abb}, \,\ldots}$
    \\
    $\eclass_{\FSM,1}=\set{\str{a}, \str{aa},\str{ba}, \str{aba},\,\ldots}$
    \\
    $\eclass_{\FSM,2}=\set{\str{ab}, \str{aab}, \str{abb},\,\ldots}$
  \end{tabular}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa21.pdf}}
\end{center}

% Make an exercise:
% The first part, $\eclass_0$, are those strings that are either
% empty, or consist of a single~\str{b}, or end in \str{bb}.
% Put another way, $\eclass_0$ is described by the regular expression
% \re{$\emptystring$|b|((a|b)*bb)}.
% Similarly, $\eclass_1$ is described by \re{(a|b)*a}
% and $\eclass_2$ is described by \re{(a|b)*ab}.

\begin{definition}
  Let $\FSM$ be a Finite State machine with alphabet~$\Sigma$.
  Two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ are
  \definend{$\FSM$-related} if starting the machine with input~$\sigma_0$
  ends with it in the same state as does starting the machine
  with input~$\sigma_1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
  The binary relation of $\FSM$-related is an equivalence,
  and so partitions the collection of all strings $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$
  into equivalence classes.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  We must show that the relation  is reflexive, symmetric,
  and transitive.
  Reflexivity, that any input string $\sigma$ brings the machine
  to the same state as itself, is obvious.
  So is symmetry, that if $\sigma_0$ brings the machine to the same state
  as $\sigma_1$ then $\sigma_1$ brings it to the same state as~$\sigma_0$.
  Transitivity is straightforward: if $\sigma_0$ brings $\FSM$ to the same state
  as $\sigma_1$, and $\sigma_1$ brings it to the same state as~$\sigma_2$,
  then $\sigma_0$ brings it to the same state as $\sigma_2$.
\end{proof}

So a machine gives rise to a partition.
Does it go the other way?

\begin{definition}
  Suppose that $\lang$ is a language over $\Sigma$.
  Two strings $\sigma,\hat{\sigma}\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$
  are \definend{$\lang$-related}
  (or \definend{$\lang$-indistinguishable}),
  denoted \definend{$\sigma\sim_{\lang}\hat{\sigma}$},
  when for every suffix $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ we have
  $\sigma\concat\tau\in\lang$ if and only if
  $\hat{\sigma}\concat\tau\in\lang$.
  Otherwise, the two strings are \definend{$\lang$-distinguishable}.
\end{definition}

Said another way, the two strings $\sigma$ and~$\hat{\sigma}$
can be $\lang$-distinguished when there is a
suffix $\tau$ that separates them:~of the two
$\sigma\concat\tau$ and $\hat{\sigma}\concat\tau$, one
is an element of~$\lang$ while the other is not.


\begin{lemma}
  For any language~$\lang$,
  the binary relation $\sim_{\lang}$
  is an equivalence, and thus gives rise to a partition of all strings.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Reflexivity, that $\sigma\sim_{\lang}\sigma$, is trivial.
  So is symmetry, that $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$
  implies $\sigma_1\sim_{\lang}\sigma_0$.
  For transitivity 
  suppose $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$ and
  $\sigma_1\sim_{\lang}\sigma_2$.
  If $\sigma_o\concat\tau\in\lang$ then by the first supposition
  $\sigma_1\concat\tau\in\lang$, and the second supposition in turn gives
  $\sigma_2\concat\tau\in\lang$.
  Similarly $\sigma_o\concat\tau\notin\lang$ implies that 
  $\sigma_2\concat\tau\notin\lang$.
  Thus $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
  Let $\lang$ be the set
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has an even number of \str{1}'s}}$.
  We can find the parts of the partition.
  If two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1$ both have an even number of \str{1}'s
  then they are $\lang$-related.
  That's because for any $\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}$, if $\tau$ has an even number of \str{1}'s then $\sigma_0\concat\tau\in\lang$ and  $\sigma_0\concat\tau\in\lang$, while if $\tau$ has an odd number of \str{1}'s then the concatenations will not be members of~$\lang$.  
  Similarly, two strings both have an odd number of
  $1$'s then they are $\lang$-related.
  So the relationship $\sim_{\lang}$ gives rise to this partition
  of $\kleenestar{\B}$.
  \begin{equation*}
    \eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\emptystring, \str{0},\str{00},\str{11},\str{000},\str{011},\str{101},\str{110},\,\ldots}
    \quad
    \eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\str{1}, \str{01},\str{10},\str{001},\str{010},\,\ldots}
  \end{equation*}  
\end{example}

\begin{example}
  Let $\lang$ be 
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}} \suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has the same number of \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s}}$.
  Then two members of~$\lang$, 
  two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ with the same number of
  \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s, are $\lang$-related.
  This is because for any
  suffix~$\tau$, the string $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ is an element of~$\lang$
  if and only if $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ is an element of~$\lang$, which
  happens if and only if $\tau$ has the same number of \str{a}'s as~\str{b}'s.

  Similarly, two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1$ such that the number of
  \str{a}'s is one more than the number of \str{b}'s are $\lang$-related
  because for any
  suffix~$\tau$, the string $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ is an element of~$\lang$
  if and only if $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ is an element of~$\lang$, namely 
  if and only if $\tau$ has one fewer \str{a} than~\str{b}.

  Following this reasoning, $\sim_{\lang}$ partitions
  $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$ into the infinitely many parts
  $\eclass_{\lang,i}=\set{\sigma\in \kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \text{the number of \str{a}'s minus the number of \str{b}'s equals $i$}}$, where $i\in\Z$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:MNMachineComparePartitions}
  This machine~$\FSM$ accepts
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has even length}}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa19.pdf}
  \end{center}
  We will compare the partitions induced by the two relations
  introduced above.

  The $\FSM$-related relation breaks $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$
  into five parts, one for each state (since each state in $\FSM$ is
  reachable).
  \begin{align*}
    \eclass_{\FSM,0} &=\set{\emptystring}  \\
    \eclass_{\FSM,1} &=\set{\str{a},\str{aaa},\str{aab},\str{aba},\str{abb},\str{aaaaa},\str{aaaab},\,\ldots}
    \\
    \eclass_{\FSM,2} &=\set{\str{b},\str{baa},\str{bab},\str{bba},\str{bbb},\str{baaaa},\str{baaab},\,\ldots}
    \\
    \eclass_{\FSM,3} &=\set{\str{aa},\str{ab},\str{aaaa},\str{aaab},\str{aaba},\str{aabb},\str{abaa},\str{abab},\str{abba},\str{abbb},\str{aaaaaa},\,\ldots{}}
    \\
    \eclass_{\FSM,4} &=\set{\str{ba},\str{bb},\str{baaa},\str{baab},\str{baba},\str{babb},\str{bbaa},\str{bbab},\str{bbba},\str{bbbb},\str{baaaaa},\,\ldots{}}
  \end{align*}
  % Make this an exercise 
  % \begin{align*}
  %   \eclass_{\FSM,0} &=\set{\emptystring}  \\
  %   \eclass_{\FSM,1} &=\set{\sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ matches \re{a((b|a)(b|a))*}}}
  %   \\
  %   \eclass_{\FSM,2} &=\set{\sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ matches \re{b((b|a)(b|a))*}}}
  %   \\
  %   \eclass_{\FSM,3} &=\set{\sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ matches \re{(aa|ab)((b|a)(b|a))*}}}
  %   \\
  %   \eclass_{\FSM,4} &=\set{\sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ matches \re{(ba|bb)((b|a)(b|a))*}}}
  % \end{align*}
  
  The $\lang$-related relation breaks $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$
  into two parts.
  \begin{equation*}
    \eclass_{\lang,0} =\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ has even length}}
    \quad
    \eclass_{\lang,1} =\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ has odd length}} 
  \end{equation*}
  Verify this by noting that if two strings are in $\eclass_{\lang,0}$
  then adding a suffix~$\tau$ will result in a string that is a member
  of~$\lang$ if and only if the length of~$\tau$ is even,
  and the same reasoning holds for
  $\eclass_{\lang,1}$ and odd-length $\tau$'s.

  The sketch below shows the universe of strings
  $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$, partitioned in two ways.
  There are two $\lang$-related parts, the left and right halves.
  The five $\FSM$-related parts are subsets of the $\lang$-related parts.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa20.pdf}
  \end{center}
  That is, the $\FSM$-related partition is finer than the
  $\lang$-related partition
  (`fine' in the sense that sand is finer than gravel).
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:MRelatedImpliesLRelated}
  Let $\FSM$ be a Finite State machine that accepts~$\lang$.
  If two strings are $\FSM$-related then they are $\lang$-related.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume that $\sigma_0$ and $\sigma_1$ are  $\FSM$-related, so that  
starting $\FSM$ with input $\sigma_0$
causes it to end in the same state as starting it with input~$\sigma_1$.
Thus for any suffix~$\tau$, giving $\FSM$ the input $\sigma_0\concat\tau$
causes it to end in the same state as does the input  $\sigma_1\concat\tau$.
In particular, $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ takes $\FSM$
to a final state if and only if $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ does.
So the two strings are $\lang$-related.  
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma} \label{lem:MNPriorThm}
  Let $\lang$ be a language.
  \begin{parenum}
  \item If two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1$ are $\lang$-related,
    $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$, then adjoining a common
    extension~$\beta$ gives strings that are also
    $\lang$-related,
    $\sigma_0\concat \beta\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1\concat \beta$.
  \item If one member of a part $\sigma_0\in\eclass_{\lang,i}$
    is an element of~$\lang$ then every member
    of that part $\sigma_1\in\eclass_{\lang,i}$ is also an element
    of~$\lang$.
  \end{parenum}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  For~(1), start with two strings
  $\sigma_0,\sigma_1$ that are $\lang$-related.
  By definition, no extension~$\tau$ will $\lang$-distinguish the
  two\Dash it is not the case that one of
  $\sigma_0\concat\tau,\sigma_1\concat\tau$
  is in $\lang$ while the other is not.
  Taking $\beta\concat\hat{\tau}$ for $\tau$ gives that for the two strings
  $\sigma_0\concat \beta$ and $\sigma_1\concat \beta$,
  no extension~$\hat{\tau}$ will $\lang$-distinguish the two.
  So they are $\lang$-related.

  Item~(2) is even easier: if $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$
  and $\sigma_0\in\lang$
  but $\sigma_1\notin\lang$ then they are distinguished by the empty string,
  which contradicts that they are $\lang$-related.
\end{proof}

\begin{example} \label{ex:MNMachineComparePartitionsCont}
  We will milk \cref{ex:MNMachineComparePartitions} for another observation.
  Take a string $\sigma$ from $\eclass_{\FSM,1}$ and append an~\str{a}.
  The result $\sigma\concat\str{a}$ is a member of
  $\eclass_{\FSM,3}$,
  simply because if the machine is in state~$q_1$ and it
  receives an~$\str{a}$ then it moves to state~$q_3$.
  Likewise, if $\sigma\in\eclass_{\FSM,4}$, then
  $\sigma\concat\str{b}$ is a member of~$\eclass_{\FSM,2}$.
  If adding the alphabet character~$x\in\Sigma$ to one
  string $\sigma$ from~$\eclass_{\lang,i}$ results in a string
  $\sigma\concat x$ from $\eclass_{\lang,j}$ then the same will happen
  for any string from~$\eclass_{\lang,i}$.
  
  In this example we see that's true because
  the $\eclass_{\FSM}$'s are contained in the $\eclass_{\lang}$'s.
  The key step of
  the next result is to find it even in a context where there is no
  machine.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Myhill-Nerode]
A language~$\lang$ is regular if and only if the relation
$\sim_{\lang}$ has only finitely many equivalence classes.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
One direction is easy.
Suppose that $\lang$ is a regular language.
Then it is accepted by a Finite State machine $\FSM$.
By \cref{lem:MRelatedImpliesLRelated}
the number of element in the partition induced by
$\sim_{\lang}$ is finite
because the number of elements in the partition
associated with being $\FSM$-related is finite, as there is one part for
each of $\FSM$'s reachable states.

For the other direction
suppose that the number of elements in the partition
associated with being $\lang$-related is finite.
We will show that $\lang$ is regular
by producing a Finite State machine
that accepts~$\lang$.

The machine's states are the partition's elements, the  $\eclass_{\lang,i}$'s.
That is, $s_i$ is $\eclass_{\lang,i}$.
The start state is the part containing the empty
string~$\emptystring$.
A state is final if that part contains 
strings from the language~$\lang$
(\cref{lem:MNPriorThm}~(2) says that each part
contains either no strings from~$\lang$ or consists entirely of strings
from~$\lang$).

The transition function is:~for any state~$s_i=\eclass_{\lang,i}$
and alphabet element~$x$,
compute the next state $\Delta(s_i,x)$ by starting with any string in that part
$\sigma\in \eclass_{\lang,i}$,
appending the character to get a new string~$\hat{\sigma}=\sigma\concat x$,
and then finding the part containing that
string, the $\eclass_{\lang,j}$ such that
$\hat{\sigma}\in\eclass_{\lang,j}$.
Then $\Delta(s_i,x)=s_j$.

We must verify that this transition function is well-defined.
That is, the definition of $\Delta(s_i,x)$ as given potentially depends on
which string $\sigma$ you choose from $s_i=\eclass_{\lang,i}$, and we must
check that choosing a different string
cannot lead to a different resulting part.
This follows from~(1) in \cref{lem:MNPriorThm}:
take two starting strings from the same part
$\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\eclass_{\lang,i}$ and make a common extension
by the one-element string~$\beta=\sequence{x}$
so the results are in the same part $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$.

Here is an equivalent way to describe the next-state function
that is illuminating.
Recall that we write the part containing $\sigma$ as $\eqclass{\sigma}$.
Then the defintion of the transition function for the machine under
construction is $\Delta(\eqclass{\sigma},x)=\eqclass{\sigma\concat{x}}$.
With that, a simple induction shows that the extended transition function
in the new machine is $\extended{\Delta}(\alpha)=\eqclass{\alpha}$.

Finally, we must verify that the language accepted by this machine
is~$\lang$.
For any string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$, starting this machine
with $\sigma$ as input will cause the machine to end in the partition
containing~$\sigma$; this is what the prior paragraph says.
This string will be accepted by this machine if and only if $\sigma\in\lang$.
\end{proof}

% In contrast with Turing machines, we can tell if two Finite
% State machines accept the same language.


\begin{exercises}
% https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/89689/78536

\begin{exercise}
Find the $\lang$ equivalence classes for each regular set.
The alphabet is $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang_0=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\suchthat n\in\N}$
\item $\lang_1=\set{\str{a}^2\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language describe the $\lang$~equivalence classes.
The alphabet is $\B$.
\begin{exparts}
\item The set of strings ending in \str{01}
\item The set of strings where every \str{0} is immediately followed by two
\str{1}'s
\item The set of string with the substring \str{0110}
\item The set of strings without the substring \str{0110}
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
The language of palindromes  
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \reversal{\sigma}=\sigma$
is not regular.
Find infinitely many $\lang$~equivalence classes.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Use the Myhill-Nerode Theorem to show that the language
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$ is not regular.
\begin{answer}
If $k\neq n$ then $\str{a}^k\str{b}^k\in\lang$
but $\str{a}^m\str{b}^k\notin\lang$, so
there are infinitely many classes. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/30722
% \end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
